## Page 1


Morse flow categories as exit path categories
Colin Fourel 1
Abstract. We prove that the topological flow category M arising from a Morse-Smale pair (f, ξ) on a
smooth closed manifold X is equivalent, as an ∞-category, to Lurie’s ∞-category SingA(X) of exit paths in
X with respect to the stratification by the stable manifolds of ξ.
The objects of M are the critical points of f, and for every pair of critical points, the space of morphisms
of M between these is the space of possibly broken trajectories of ξ connecting them; it can be identified
up to homotopy with the space of unbroken ones. The latter maps naturally to the space of exit paths
connecting these critical points; we prove this map to be a weak homotopy equivalence. Then, we combine
these ingredients with several others to construct a zigzag of equivalences between the homotopy coherent
nerve of M, denoted N(M), and SingA(X). The n-simplices of N(M) are homotopy coherent diagrams of
n composable morphisms of M; we introduce the notion of unbroken diagram, yielding an ∞-subcategory of
N(M), which we refer to as the flow coherent nerve of M. The simplices of the latter give rise to stratified
maps out of a family of stratified cubes, into X. We organize this family into a functor from the category of
finite ordered sequences of critical points, to the category of A-stratified topological spaces, and we prove a
comparison result with the usual stratified geometric realization functor. We finally use a theorem of Tanaka
that associates a functor of ∞-categories to a map a semi-simplicial sets satisfying some conditions.
Our theorem has implications regarding constructible sheaves and the description of homotopy types in
terms of flow categories.
Reader’s guide. After recalling the definitions of the main objects and stating the main result in section
1.1, we present some motivations and consequence in section 1.2, we briefly present the idea of the proof in
section 1.3 and we give a detailed summary of the proof in section 1.4. This paper offers two perspectives on
the same proof. The first perspective, which is the proof in itself, consists of sections 2 through 8. On the
other hand, our theorem is a consequence of a more general statement which does not refer to Morse flow
categories, but instead to what we call stratified categories. This second perspective is presented in section
9; this section is designed to be directly accessible to the reader with a good familiarity with the basics of
(∞, 1)-category theory (as developed in [Lur09]), stratified homotopy theory and Morse flow categories. The
necessary background on these topics is presented in section 2.

Introduction
1.1
Main objects and main result
Consider a closed smooth manifold X together with a Morse function f : X →R, and denote by A the set
of critical points of f. Let ξ be a negative Morse-Smale pseudo-gradient vector field on X adapted to f 2.
Such a datum (f, ξ) will be called a Morse-Smale pair on X.
The central object of study of this paper is the flow category of (X, f, ξ), denoted M. It is defined as follows:
• The set of objects of M is A, the set of critical points of f.
• Given two critical points a and b, M(a, b) is the set of possibly broken, and unparametrized, trajectories
of ξ linking a to b.
• The composition law is defined as the concatenation of trajectories of ξ.
Example 1.1.1. Let us illustrate this construction with figures 1 and 2.
Figure 1: The manifold X is the image of an embedding of S2 in R3 and f is the
restriction to X of the projection to the vertical axis. It has four critical points; one
maximum a, one local maximum c, one saddle point b and one minimum d. The vector
field ξ on X is defined to be the negative gradient of f with respect to the restriction
to X of the Euclidean metric on R3.
2Although we will prove the main result of this paper under this assumption on ξ, one can think of ξ, as a first approximation
in this introduction, as the negative gradient of f for some Riemannian metric on X.
4


## Page 5


Figure 2: On the left: the only trajectory of ξ from a to b. In the middle: the two
trajectories from b to d. On the right: some unbroken trajectories from a to d. In
addition to these, there are two broken trajectories from a to d, obtained by composing
the one from a to b with the two from b to d.
We say that these two trajectories
are broken at b.
This allows to identify M(a, d) with the closed interval [0, 1], by
identifying the two boundary components of [0, 1] with the two broken trajectories
from a to d, and by identifying the interior of [0, 1] with the one-parameter family of
unbroken trajectories connecting the two broken ones. Similarly, M(c, b) consists of one
morphism only, and M(c, d) can be identified with [0, 1]. Finally, all the endomorphism
sets of M are reduced to the constant trajectory at the corresponding critical points,
and all the other morphism sets of M are empty. This gives a complete description of
the category M in that case.
One can equip the morphism sets of M with several structures compatible with the composition operation
in M. In this paper, we will mainly view M as a topological category. We will adopt the following definition
of topological category.
Definition 1.1.2. A topological category is a category enriched over the category of (compactly generated
and weakly Hausdorff) topological spaces.
We defer to later the precise description of the topology on the morphism sets of M (see section 2.4.3). For
the moment we just describe it in the case of example 1.1.1.
Example 1.1.1 (continued). The topology on M(b, d) is the discrete topology, and the topology on
M(a, d) is identified with the usual topology on the closed interval [0, 1]. Similarly, the topology on M(c, d)
is identified with the usual topology on [0, 1].
Topological categories form one model for the theory of (∞, 1)-categories, and the goal of this paper is to
understand M as an ∞-category.
To this end we will consider a stratification of X determined by (f, ξ). We will define later what we mean
by a stratification (see section 2.3.1), for the moment one can just think of this stratification as a partition
of X indexed by the set of critical points of f (which we denote by A)

Example 1.1.3. In the case of example 1.1.1, this stratification is as in figure 3.
Figure 3: The strata associated with the two local maxima a and c are reduced to
the respective critical point; indeed, the only way for a trajectory of ξ to converge to a

To every unbroken trajectory γ of ξ is associated a path in X, whose endpoints are the critical points
lim
t→−∞γ(t)
and
lim
t→+∞γ(t).
The paths obtained in this way fall into the following specific class of paths associated with the stratification
by the stable manifolds of ξ.
Definition 1.1.4. An exit path is a path δ such that δ((0, 1]) is contained in a single stratum.
Figure 4: An exit path δ in a stratified space Y with two strata Y0 and Y1.
The class of exit paths is not closed under concatenation in general, since the concatenation of two exit
paths that each visit two strata, will visit three strata, which an exit path cannot.
However, for every
6


## Page 7


stratified topological space satisfying a certain regularity condition — namely, being conically stratified —
Lurie ([Lur17, Appendix A]) constructed an ∞-category satisfying the following property
ß Objects
= points of the stratified space
Hom(x, y)
= space of exit paths from x to y.
This ∞-category is referred to as the ∞-category of exit paths in the stratification, or the exit path ∞-category
associated with the stratification
By a theorem of Nicolaescu (theorem 3.2.3), the stratification on X that we are interested in satisfies
Whitney’s regularity condition (b) and therefore belongs to the class of conically stratified spaces. The main
theorem of this paper is the following:
Theorem 1. The ∞-category defined by the topological category M is equivalent to the exit path ∞-category
associated with the stratification of X by the stable manifolds of ξ.
The precise formulation of this theorem as well as additional properties of the equivalence that we construct
will be given in section 2.5, after we have set up the necessary preliminaries in sections 2.1 through 2.4.
In the next section, we present some motivations for this theorem and some of its consequences.

1.2.1
Morse homology and constructible sheaves

However, not every functor G : π0(M) →ModZ/2Z is obtained by taking the monodromy of a local system;
indeed, a necessary condition for this to hold is that for every a, b ∈A and γ ∈M(a, b), the morphism
G(γ) : G(a) →G(b) is an isomorphism. As long as there exists a non-constant trajectory of ξ (i.e. as long
as X has dimension at least 1), there exists a functor G that does not satisfy this property.
The above discussion was formulated in the setting of mod 2 coefficients in order to keep it as simple as

• How can we describe the category Fun(π0(M), ModZ)?
This construction and these questions are 1-categorical versions of analogous constructions and questions
formulated at the ∞-categorical level, as we now briefly explain. In [BDHO25], Barraud, Damian, Humilière
and Oancea introduced Morse homology with differential graded coefficients as a generalization of classical
Morse homology. Assume X is connected and fix a basepoint in X. The category of coefficients of this
theory is the category of differential graded (DG) modules over the DG algebra of cubical chains on the
space of based Moore loops on X, denoted C∗(ΩX). This category can be described as the category of
∞-local systems on X, i.e. the category of representations over Z of the ∞-groupoid associated with X (this
is discussed in [Hol15, Theorem 26] and [PT22, Corollary 1.5]). In order to present the analogue of the above
construction in this context, consider the differential graded category C∗(M) defined as

is defined for every a, b, c ∈M as:
C∗(M(a, b)) ⊗C∗(M(b, c))

## Page 9


where EZ is the Eilenberg-Zilber map and c∗is the map induced at the chain level by the composition
operation at the level of morphism spaces of M.
The construction of Morse homology with DG coeffiients generalizes naturally to the category of DG modules
over C∗(M). By a DG module over C∗(M) we mean a differential graded functor F : C∗(M) →ChZ, where
ChZ denotes the DG category of chain complexes of abelian groups.
Equivalently, a DG module over
C∗(M) is the datum of a chain complex F(a) for every a ∈M, and of a morphism of chain complexes
F(a) ⊗C∗(M(a, b)) →F(b) for every a, b ∈M, such that the identity morphisms in C∗(M) induce identity
morphisms in ChZ, and associativity holds (in the strict sense).
To summarize, once (f, ξ) is fixed, the natural domain of coefficients for Morse homology over Z seems to be
the category Fun(π0(M), ModZ) and, more generally, the natural domain of coefficients for Morse homology
with DG coefficients over Z seems to be the category of DG modules over C∗(M). The following will be
consequences of our main result combined with Lurie’s work on exit path categories, and will be proven in
section 8.2.
Corollary 1.2.1. The category Fun(π0(X), ModZ) is equivalent to the category of sheaves of abelian groups
on X that are constructible with respect to the stratification by the stable manifolds of ξ (i.e., that are locally
constant in restriction to each stratum).
Corollary 1.2.2. The functor m : LocX →Fun(π0(M), ModZ) induces an equivalence between LocX and
the full subcategory of the right-hand side on those functors that send every morphism to an isomorphism.
Corollary 1.2.2 is a special case of a more general statement. In order to explain this, consider the topological
category Π∞(X) defined as
Π∞(X) =
ß Objects
= points of X,

= space of (Moore) paths from x to y.
Associating to a critical point of f the corresponding point of X, and to a (possibly broken) trajectory of ξ
between x and y the corresponding path in X defines a functor of topological categories
M →Π∞(X).
Consider further the category Π1(X) = π0(Π∞(X)) defined from the topological category Π∞(X) by taking
π0 of the morphism spaces.
This is the category whose objects are the points of X, and whose sets of
morphisms are the sets of homotopy classes of paths with fixed extremities; it is called the fundamental
groupoid of X. The functor M →Π∞(X) thus determines a functor π0(M) →Π1(X).
Taking the monodromy along paths determines an equivalence of categories LocX

Corollary 1.2.3. The functor π0(M) →Π1(X) exhibits Π1(X) as the localization of π0(M) with respect to
the class of all morphisms. In other words, for every category C the restriction functor
Fun(Π1(X), C) →Fun(π0(M), C)
induces an equivalence between the left-hand side and the full subcategory of the right-hand side on those
functors that send every morphism to an isomorphism.
9


## Page 10


At the ∞-categorical level we have the following results.
Corollary 1.2.4. The ∞-category of functors from the ∞-category associated with M to the derived ∞-
category D(Z) is equivalent to the ∞-category of sheaves on X with values in D(Z) that are constructible
with respect to the stratification by the stable manifolds of ξ.
In future work, we plan to provide a description of this ∞-category as the derived ∞-category of DG-modules
over C∗(M). Given a DG-module over C∗(M), we plan to prove that the associated Morse complex with
DG coefficients is a model for the homotopy colimit of the corresponding D(Z)-valued constructible sheaf,
regarded as a D(Z)-valued functor from the ∞-category associated with M.
Remark 1.2.5. The formalism of Floer homotopy theory developed in [AB24] allows one to define Morse
homology with respect to a larger class of coefficients, such as the sphere spectrum. It would be interesting
to combine our main result with the results of [HO26] to define Morse homology with coefficients in a
constructible sheaf of spectra.
Finally, we have the following consequence of our main result.
Corollary 1.2.6. The functor N(M) →Sing(X), obtained by composing the equivalence N(M) →SingA(X)
provided by theorem 1 with the inclusion SingA(X) →Sing(X), exhibits the fundamental ∞-groupoid of X
as the localization of N(M) with respect to the class of all morphisms.
Corollary 1.2.6 is the ∞-categorical analogue of corollary 1.2.3 but it is less precise in that it does not describe
as explicitly the functor exhibiting the fundamental ∞-groupoid of X as the localization of the ∞-category
associated with M with respect to the class of all morphisms. We expect this functor to be the functor of
∞-categories induced by the functor of topological categories M →Π∞(X) above.
1.2.2
Flow categories and homotopy types
In [CJS95b], Cohen, Jones and Segal considered a Morse function f on a smooth closed Riemannian manifold
X. In this context, taking ξ = −∇f gives rise to a flow category M in the same way as presented in section
1.1, even without assuming Smale transversality on ξ. They also considered the classifying space construction,
which associates a topological space to every topological category. They claimed the following.
(i) The classifying space of M, denoted BM, is homotopy equivalent to X.
(ii) If ξ satisfies the Smale transversality condition then BM is homeomorphic to X.
The proof of claim (i) contains a mistake. A counterexample to claim (i) as well as a corrected proof under
some assumptions on ξ are given in [CL26]. On the other hand, the proof of claim (ii) relies on the existence
of structures of smooth manifolds with corners on the morphism spaces of M, which were proven later by Qin
([Qin10, Theorem 3.3]) under the further assumption that the metric is locally trivial, in other words, under
the same assumptions on ξ as the ones we make in this paper (see section 2.4 for the precise formulation of
these assumptions). The proof of (ii) also relies on an unproven lemma ([CJS95b, Lemma 3.5]), which is a
consequence of one of our theorems (theorem 6.2.1, see also remark 6.2.2).
Now suppose that ξ satisfies the hypotheses of the main theorem of this paper. Under this assumption,
our results thus complete the proof of claim (ii), and therefore also that of claim (i). However a different
approach to claim (i) is possible, as we now explain. To every ∞-category is associated a ∞-groupoid (i.e.,
an ∞-category all of whose morphisms are invertible) called the group completion, obtained by formally
inverting all morphisms. The homotopy theory of ∞-groupoids is equivalent to the homotopy theory of
CW-complexes via the fundamental ∞-groupoid construction and according to corollary 1.2.6, the group
completion of the ∞-category associated with M is the homotopy type of the manifold X.

Given a topological category C , it is not always the case that its classifying space and the group completion
of its associated ∞-category determine the same homotopy type. However, we will prove in [Fou] that this
is the case for M, thus recovering (i) from our main theorem.
To finish this section, we would like to make a simple observation showing that the ∞-category associated
with M contains more information than the homotopy type of X. To this end, suppose we have another
Morse-Smale pair (f′, ξ′) on X and let us denote by M′ the associated flow category. We then have two
∞-categories whose group completions are the same.
(∞, 1) −Categories

Here we denoted by [M], [M′] the ∞-categories associated to M and M′ respectively, by [X] the homotopy
type of X, and the left vertical arrow is the group completion functor.
However, it is not the case in general that M and M′ are equivalent as ∞-categories, as we now discuss.
Example 1.2.7. Let us consider the Morse-Smale pair on S2 described in figure 5.
Figure 5: The Morse function is the restriction to the round 2-sphere S2 ⊂R3 of the
projection to the vertical axis. It has two critical points, on maximum and one mini-
mum. The Smale pseudo-gradient vector field is the negative gradient of the function
with respect to the restriction to S2 of the Euclidean metric on R3. Mapping a point
of the equator of S2 to the trajectory that passes through it defines a homeomorphism
between S1 and the morphism space of the flow category between the maximum and
the minimum.
The characterization of equivalences of ordinary categories as functors which are essentially surjective and
fully faithful extends to the (∞, 1)-categorical setting. In the (∞, 1)-categorical world, the full faithfulness
condition is the condition that the map induced between morphism spaces is a weak homotopy equivalence.
The definition of essential surjectivity in the ∞-categorical setting will be given in section 2.2. The flow
category from example 1.2.7 has a morphism space which is homeomorphic to S1, while none of the morphism
spaces of the flow category from example 1.1.1 are weakly homotopy equivalent to S1. There can therefore
not exist a fully faithful functor between the ∞-categories defined by these two flow categories, and in
particular these two ∞-categories are not equivalent.
It is therefore natural to ask whether M, as an ∞-category, reflect some extra structure on X. Theorem 1
can be regarded as an answer to this question.

1.3
Idea of the proof and organization of the paper
Let us explain briefly the principle of our proof. We regard ∞-categories as those simplicial sets that satisfy
the inner horn filling property. The ∞-category defined by the topological category M is the homotopy
coherent nerve of M, denoted N(M). The exit path ∞-category associated with the stratification of X by
the stable manifolds of ξ is the stratified singular simplicial set of X, denoted SingA(X). For small values
of n, these two simplicial sets are described and compared as follows.
n = 0: The 0-simplices of SingA(X) are the points of X. On the other hand, the 0-simplices of N(M) are
the objects of M, i.e., the critical points of f. There is therefore an inclusion
N(M)0 ⊂SingA(X)0.

unital, i.e., that preserves identity morphisms only up to homotopy. Fortunately, the results of [Tan18]
guarantee the existence of a genuine functor that we denote here i2.
We prove that i1 and i2 are both equivalences of ∞-categories, by proving that these are essentially surjective
and fully faithful. The idea behind the full faithfulness is the following. Given two critical points a and b,
passing to the morphism spaces between a and b, the above zigzag becomes:

Now, the maps i1(a, b) and i2(a, b) correspond to the natural inclusions, both of which we will prove to be
weak homotopy equivalences.
The paper is organized as follows. Section 1.4 consist of a very detailed summary of our proof. It does
not contain precise constructions and proofs, but rather attempts to explain the logic underlying the overall
progression of the proof, as well as the constructions and results it comprises.
The remainder of the paper consists of carrying out the constructions and completing the arguments. Section
2 is a brief introduction to (∞, 1)-category theory, stratified homotopy theory and Morse flow categories.
We recommend to consult it depending on the reader’s background. In section 3, we review the theory of
Whitney stratifications and use it to prove that the map i2(a, b) above is a weak homotopy equivalence. In
section 4, we study the homotopy coherent nerve functor on the category of topological categories. As is
already apparent in the sketch of proof given above, the simplices of the homotopy coherent nerve of M
naturally give rise to higher rectangles in X, not simplices on the nose. In section 5, we construct from these
higher rectangles an alternative to the usual stratified geometric realization functor, and give the definition
of the flow coherent nerve of M. In section 6, we prove a comparison result between the two geometric
realizations. This can be understood as an instance of the comparison between the theory of simplicial sets
and the theory of cubical sets. Although a mere isomorphism between these functors does not exist, our
result is sufficient for our purposes, thanks to a theorem of Tanaka that we review in section 8.2. In section
7, we prove that SM is an ∞-category and, roughly, that the two functors i1 and i2 above are equivalences.
In section 9, we give a general framework to which our methods extend. We prove a generalization of our
main result where Morse flow categories are replaced by another class of objects called stratified categories
inspired from them, which we introduce.
1.4
Detailed summary of the proof
The exit path ∞-category construction is defined by Lurie in the model of simplicial sets for ∞-categories.
It is modeled by a simplicial set called the stratified singular simplicial set of the stratified space X, and
denoted SingA(X) 3. Our strategy is to first bring M in the world of simplicial sets. This is done through
3Note that there is an abuse of notation here, since this simplicial depends on more than simply the topological space X
and the set A.
14


## Page 15


the homotopy coherent nerve functor
N : CatTop −→sSet
from the category of topological categories to the category of simplicial sets, which identifies the theory of ∞-
categories formulated in the language of topological categories, with the theory of ∞-categories formulated
in the language of simplicial sets. In other words, the ∞-category defined by M is the same as that defined
by N(M). We are thus reduced to comparing the simplicial sets N(M) and SingA(X). Our proof consists
in constructing a zigzag of morphisms of simplicial sets that are equivalences of ∞-categories
N(M) ←SM →SingCA,r(X) →Sing′

In this section, we summarize the construction of these ∞-categories and equivalences.
To begin with, let us describe the simplicial set SingA(X). This is a simplicial subset of Sing(X), that is,
the n-simplices of SingA(X) are some particular singular n-simplices in X. Let us describe them for n going
from 0 to 3 (complete definitions are given in section 2.3).
n = 0: SingA(X)0 = Sing(X)0, in other words, the 0-simplices of SingA(X) are the points of X.
n = 1: The 1-simplices of SingA(X) are the exit paths in X. In other words, a 1-simplex of SingA(X) is the
datum of a sequence of two (not necessarily different) critical points a0, a1 and a path |∆1| →X that is
compatible with the stratification of |∆1| by {a0, a1} described in figure 8.

Let us clarifiy that, by "compatible with the stratification", we mean that the a0-stratum (resp. a1-stratum)
of |∆1| is mapped to the a0-stratum (resp. a1-stratum) of X. We indeed recover the definition of an exit
path in this way.
Before moving on to higher n, we would like to point out that given two critical points a0 and a1, there does
not always exist an exit path starting in the a0-stratum and ending in the a1-stratum. It is useful to endow
A with the relation ≤defined as a0 ≤a1 if and only if such an exit path exists (equivalent characterizations
of this condition are given in corollary 3.3.6). As the notation suggests, this turns out to be a partial order.
n = 2: A 2-simplex of SingA(X) is the datum of a sequence of three (not necessarily distinct) critical points
a0, a1, a2 and a singular 2-simplex |∆2| →X that is compatible with the stratification of |∆2| by {a0, a1, a2}

In order to compare SingA(X) with N(M), we need to understand what N(M) is. The functor N admits

For every nonnegative integer n, describing explicitly what are the n-simplicies of the homotopy coherent

One can see fσ as an n-dimensional singular cube of X 5. Recall that our goal is to compare N(M) and
SingA(X). We would therefore wish to associate to fσ an n-simplex of SingA(X), that is, a stratified singular
n-simplex in X. Moreover, the ith vertex of this simplex should be the critical point ai. But observe that,
by definition of SingA(X), such an n-simplex can only visit the ai-strata for 0 ≤i ≤n. In comparison,
the presence of broken trajectories in the morphism spaces of M has the effect that one does not have such
control on the strata which the map fσ can visit. Consider for example the 1-simplex of N(M) described
in figure 11.

More precisely, that map fσ has to visit at least the ai-strata for 0 ≤i ≤n.
Indeed, by functoriality
of σ : F(∆n) →M, the image by σ(0, n) of the morphism of F(∆n) between 0 and n obtained as the
composition
∗≃F(∆n)(0, 1) × F(∆n)(1, 2) × . . . × F(∆n)(n −1, n) →F(∆n)(0, n)
is broken at least at each of the ai, and therefore the ai’s belong to the image of fσ.
The condition preventing fσ from visiting "too many" strata can be formulated as follows: we should restrict
ourselves to those σ that map morphisms of F(∆n) to trajectories that are not broken more times than
imposed by the functoriality of σ.
This condition is introduced in section 5. We formulate it here in a different, but equivalent way. For all
finite increasing sequence of critical points a0 ≤. . . ≤an ∈A, we define a stratification of the cube In−1 ×
[f(an), f(a0)] by {a0, . . . , an} 6 such that the restrictive condition we want on σ ∈N(M)n is implemented
by the requirement that fσ is compatible with the respective stratifications of In−1 × [f(an), f(a0)] and X.
By "compatible", we mean that the ai-stratum of the cube is mapped to the ai-stratum of X for every i.
Some of these stratifications of cubes for small values of n are presented in figures 12, 13 and 14.

## Page 20


When fσ preserves the stratifications, we say that σ is unbroken.
Unbroken simplices form a simplicial
subset SM ⊂N(M), which we call the flow coherent nerve of M. We prove in section 7.1 that this is
an ∞-category. This is done using structures of smooth manifolds with corners on the morphism spaces
of M, which are presented in section 2.4. We prove in section 7.2 that the functor SM →N(M) defined
by the morphism of inclusion, is an equivalence of ∞-categories. For this, we use the characterization of
equivalences of ∞-categories as those functors that are essentially surjective and fully faithful. In turns
out that the morphism spaces of SM can be explicitly described as the spaces of unbroken trajectories
connecting critical points (see proposition 7.2.12). Now, for every pair of critical points, the inclusion of the
space of unbroken trajectories between them, into the space of all trajectories between them, is a homotopy
equivalence (see corollary 2.4.42).
Until now, we have imposed restrictions on the simplices of N(M) that we consider, with the perspective to
associate to them simplices of SingA(X), that is, stratified singular simplices of X. When the n-simplex σ
of N(M) is unbroken, the associated map fσ visits the correct strata. Moreover, unbroken simplices form
a simplicial subset SM ⊂N(M) and this inclusion is an equivalence of ∞-categories: we therefore aim to
compare the simplicial sets SM and SingA(X) from now on.
At this point, the following difficulty arises: when comparing the stratifications that we have introduced on
cubes (figures 12, 13 and 14) with the stratifications on the standard simplices (figures 8, 9 and 10), we see
that, in most cases, these are not homeomorphic. Therefore, even with this restriction imposed, fσ does not
yield a stratified singular n-simplex of X on the nose.
To solve this problem, we further notice that, by construction, the singular cubes of X arising from simplices
of the homotopy coherent nerve of the flow category, are constant on some subsets of the cube. As a basic
illustration of this, we observe that all the trajectories in the image of σ(0, n) start at a0 and end at an.
Consequently, fσ is constant equal to a0 on In−1×{f(a0)}, and is constant equal to an on In−1×{f(an)}. The
map fσ thus induces a map out of the cube In−1 ×[f(an), f(a0)] with each of the two subsets In−1 ×{f(an)}
and In−1 × {f(a0)} collapsed to a point. In the four cases of figure 13, corresponding to the case n = 2,
these are described in figure 15.

Now, when we compare figure 9 with figure 13, it appears that we have partially resolved the problem of
the difference between stratified 2-cubes and stratified 2-simplices. In the case of a sequence of three dis-
tinct critical points, the quotiented stratified 2-cube is clearly stratified homeomorphic to the corresponding
stratified 2-simplex. This is however not the case for all sequences. For example, when it comes to constant
sequences, the quotiented 2-cube is a point, while the 2-simplex is not. We will explain below how this
20

problem is solved.
More generally, for every nonnegative integer n and every increasing sequence of n + 1 critical points a0 ≤
. . . ≤an, we introduce in section 5 an equivalence relation on In−1 × [f(an), f(a0)] with which both fσ
and the stratification are compatible. The compatibility with fσ is valid whether σ is unbroken or not. In
practice, section 5 is organized as follows: we first define in section 5.1 the equivalence relations on cubes
associated with finite increasing sequences of critical points, and then we introduce the stratifications in
section 5.2.
In figures 16, 17 and 18 we describe this equivalence relation in the three cases from figure 14, as well as the
resulting quotients.

We would now like to point out that we do not merely wish to compare stratified simplices and stratified cubes
individually. We want to compare the simplicial sets SM and SingA(X), and consequently, in addition to
comparing the sets of n-simplices for every n, we also need some compatibility with the simplicial structure.
The reason why SingA(X) is a simplicial subset of Sing(X) is that the face and degeneracy maps between
standard simplices are compatible with the stratifications on these. This can be precisely formulated as
follows: the usual geometric realization functor | −| : ∆→Top lifts to a functor
| −|A : ∆A →TopA.
Here, TopA is the category of A-stratified topological spaces, and ∆A is the category of finite increasing
sequences of critical points, with arrows generated by the face and degeneracy maps. More precise definitions
of these notions are given in section 2.3. In this section, we will just use the following notation: given a finite
increasing sequence of critical points a = a0 ≤a1 ≤. . . ≤an, we denote by ∆a the corresponding object of
∆A.
In section 5, we organize stratified quotiented cubes into a functor
CA : ∆A →TopA.
This is another reason why we work with these quotients of cubes, as these quotients are essential to define
the functor CA. One cannot realize this functor as a quotient of another functor whose objectwise values
would be the cubes prior to quotienting.
We can define, thanks to CA, a simplicial set SingCA(X) in the same way as SingA(X) is defined from | −|A
and the A-stratified space X, in other words:
23


## Page 24


SingCA(X)n =
{CA(∆a) →X continuous and compatible with stratifications, for some increasing
sequence a of n + 1 critical points },
with the simplicial structure induced by the functorial structure on CA. The functor CA is constructed in
such a way that the natural assignment SM →SingCA(X) defined as σ 7→fσ, is a morphism of simplicial
sets.
At this point of our discussion towards a comparison between N(M) and SingA(X), the following questions
arise:
• Is the simplicial set SingCA(X) an ∞-category? If yes, is the functor determined by SM →SingCA(X)
an equivalence?
• To what extent can we compare the simplicial sets SingCA(X) and SingA(X)?
Note that the first question is related to the second, because we know that SingA(X) is an ∞-category.
Our strategy to address these questions can be briefly summarized as follows.
• We prove a comparison result between the functors | −|A and CA, which in turn implies a comparison
result between the simplicial sets SingCA(X) and SingA(X). This result is not a mere isomorphism,
but is sufficient for our purposes.
• We prove that the morphism SM →SingCA(X) is an equivalence of ∞-categories after corestriction
to a suitable subcategory of the target.
We now explain this in more detail. We first focus our attention on the functors | −|A and CA.
Let us summarize the similarities and differences between | −|A and CA that we have noted so far. At this
point, we have seen on the above pictures that, for n = 1, 2 and 3, and given a sequence of n distinct critical
points, the corresponding stratified n-simplex and stratified quotiented n-cube are stratified homeomorphic.
On the other hand, when the sequence is constant, the quotiented stratified cube is a point, so this statement
does not hold.
Let us take a closer look at the stratification of the standard n-simplex |∆n| defined by the constant sequence
of length n, at some critical point a0. It consists of the single a0-stratum (see figures 8 and 9 for the cases
n = 1 and n = 2). An n-simplex of SingA(X) associated with the constant sequence at a0 is therefore the
same as a singular n-simplex of the a0-stratum of X.
Roughly speaking, such simplices of SingA(X) serve to capture the homotopy types of the strata. But in our
case, the strata are contractible, so we don’t really need those kind of simplices. We can thus quotient the
functor | −|A by its restriction to constant sequences, and the resulting functor, denoted | −|′
A, is just as
good as | −|A to define the exit path ∞-category of this stratification of X.
Remark 1.4.1. Note that the endomorphism spaces of M are reduced to points, while the endomorphism
space of a point x ∈X as an object of SingA(X), is the space of loops of the stratum of x, based at
x. In particular, the condition that the strata are (weakly) contractible is necessary for an equivalence of
∞-categories between M and SingA(X) to exist.
In practice, for every ∆a ∈∆A, |∆a|′
A is obtained from |∆a|A by collapsing to a point each of the subsimplices
of |∆a|A corresponding to constant subsequences of a (the detailed construction is given as construction
6.1.1). In the cases presented above in figures 9 and 10, the results are described in figures 19 and 20.

Note that the quotient natural transformation | −|A →| −|′

Let us explain the meaning of the subscript "r" in the zigzag. For this, observe that the simplices of Sing′
A(X)
and SingCA(X) that are entirely contained in a given stratum, are constant. Said differently, the restriction
of these simplicial sets to a given stratum is always discrete. In contrast, this is not the case for SingA(X) (as
long as the stratum is of dimension at least 1). To remedy this, we introduce the restricted versions of these
two simplicial sets, denoted with a subscript "r", and defined to be the respective simplicial subsets formed
by those simplices whose vertices are critical points. This modification is harmless: using the fact that the
strata of X are contractible, we can show that the inclusion Sing′
A,r(X) →SingA(X) is an equivalence of
∞-categories (proposition 8.1.3).
Next, by construction, the morphism of simplicial sets SM →SingCA(X) corestricts to a morphism of
simplicial sets SM →SingCA,r(X). We prove in section 7.4 that this is an equivalence of ∞-categories
(proposition 7.4.5). Again, this is done by proving that it is essentially surjective and fully faithful. The
geometric reason behind the full faithfulness of this functor is the result that the inclusion of the space of
unbroken trajectories between two given critical points, into the space of exit paths between these, is a weak
homotopy equivalence. The proof of this result is the main purpose of section 3. Our proof combines a
description of the spaces of exit path in cylindrically stratified spaces (which is presented in section 2.3.4),
with cylindrical properties of Whitney stratifications (which are presented in section 3.1). It uses as an
essential ingredient a theorem of Nicolaescu ([Nic07], theorems 4.32 and 4.33) asserting that the Smale
transversality condition for the pseudo-gradient ξ is equivalent to the condition that the partition of X by
the stable manifolds of ξ is a Whitney stratification.
Lastly, it remains to compare the functors CA and |−|′
A. If these functors were isomorphic, the two simplicial
sets SingCA(X) and Sing′
A(X) would be isomorphic, and so would be the simplicial sets SingCA,r(X) and
Sing′
A,r(X), which would complete the construction of a zigzag of equivalences between N(M) and SingA(X).
However, as long as dim(X) > 0, these two functors are not isomorphic (proposition 6.1.11). In other words,
it is not possible to find a family of stratified homeomorphisms
CA(∆a) ≃|∆a|′
A
for all finite increasing sequences of critical points, which are compatible with both face and degeneracy
maps. Still, a weaker, but sufficient result, does hold. Namely, we prove that such a family of stratified
homeomorphisms exist if one requires compatibility with faces only (theorem 6.2.1). In other words, the
restrictions of these two functors to the subcategory of ∆A generated by faces, are isomorphic.
This has two essential consequences for us.
Firstly, this result suffices to imply that SingCA(X) is an
∞-category (proposition 7.3.4), and therefore also SingCA,r(X). Secondly, it implies that SingCA(X) and
Sing′
A(X) are isomorphic as semi-simplicial sets, and likewise for SingCA,r(X) and Sing′
A,r(X).
Tanaka
shows in [Tan18] how to naturally associate a functor of ∞-categories to a morphism of semi-simplicial sets
between ∞-categories, provided that the latter preserves identities up to homotopy. We discuss this result
in section 8.2. It applies in our case and yields an equivalence of ∞-categories between SingCA,r(X) and
Sing′
A,r(X). With this, the proof of our main theorem is complete.
To finish this section, we quickly outline our proof that CA and | −|′
A are isomorphic in restriction to the
subcategory generated by face morphisms. For every finite increasing sequence of critical points a of length
n, CA(∆a) (resp. |∆a|′
A) is constructed as some quotient of an n-cube (resp. an n-simplex). Both the cube
and the simplex are homeomorphic to the closed Euclidean ball Dn. The key point of our proof is to show
that this remains true after passing to the respective quotients (as long as a is not constant), and that any
26


## Page 27


homeomorphism CA(∆a) ≃Dn (resp. |∆a|′
A ≃Dn) identifies the union of the faces of CA(∆a) (resp. |∆a|′
A)
with ∂Dn = Sn−1. This result if formulated as theorem 6.2.8; although it might be intuitively clear that
such a statement holds, it takes quite some work to prove. The proof is the purpose of section 6.3. The
argument analyzes the geometry of polytopes and shows that certain quotients of polytopes are themselves
homeomorphic to polytopes. With this theorem at hand, we can then argue by induction: once a family of
homeomorphisms compatible with faces is constructed for sequences of length at most n, this family can be
extended to sequences of length at most n + 1 as follows. If a is of length n + 1 and not constant, combining
two homeomorphisms CA(∆a) ≃Dn and |∆a|′
A ≃Dn, with the family of homeomorphisms between the
faces of CA(∆a) and |∆a|′
A, available to us by the induction hypothesis, we get a homeomorphism of the
sphere Sn−1. A homeomorphism between CA(∆a) and |∆a|′
A, which is both stratified and compatible with
the faces, is then the same as an extension of this homeomorphism of Sn−1 to a homeomorphism of Dn. The
conclusion follows because such an extension exists.

2.1
Categories of presheaves, left Kan extensions and simplicial sets
In this paper, we make a very important use of the category of simplicial sets, as well as the more general
notions of categories of presheaves, and left Kan extensions to these. This section is intended to serve as a
reference about these concepts. We start by reviewing the basic properties of categories of presheaves, and
left Kan extensions to categories of presheaves. We then focus on the particular case of simplicial sets. For
a more comprehensive review, see for example [Dug99, Section 2.2] or [Rie17].
We denote by Set the category of sets.
Let C be a small category.
Definition 2.1.1. The category of presheaves on C is the functor category Fun(Cop, Set).
It is denoted
Pre(C).
Remark 2.1.2. The category Pre(C) admits all (small) limits and colimits. For every diagram D : J →
Pre(C), the limit and colimits of D are given explicitly by the formulas
(lim
D J)(C) = (lim
D J(C))
(colim

D
J(C)).
Definition 2.1.3. The Yoneda functor is the functor
r :

−→

C

HomC(−, C).
The presheaf r(C) is said to be represented by C. Objects in the essential image of r are called representables.
The following lemma is known as the Yoneda lemma:
Lemma 2.1.4. For every C ∈C and F ∈Pre(C), the correspondence
HomPre(C)(r(C), F)
−→
F(C)
φ
7−→
φ(C)(idC)
is a bijection. In particular, r is fully faithful.
An important example for us is the special case of the category of simplicial sets, which we now introduce.
Notation 2.1.5. We adopt the following notation about partially ordered sets (posets).
• We denote by Pos the category of posets. Recall that this is the category whose objects are posets,
and whose morphisms are order-preserving maps between posets.
• Given n ≥0, we denote by [n] the ordered set 0 < 1 < . . . < n, which we regard as an object of Pos.
Definition 2.1.6. The simplex category ∆is the full subcategory of Pos whose objects are the posets [n],
for n ranging through nonnegative integers. Given an integer n ≥0, we denote by ∆n the object of ∆
corresponding to the poset [n].
Definition 2.1.7. A simplicial set is an object of the category of presheaves Pre(∆).
This category of
presheaves is called the category of simplicial sets and denoted sSet.
28


## Page 29


Let us now introduce some notation and terminology about simplicial sets.
Notation and Terminology 2.1.8. Let n be a nonnegative integer and 0 ≤i ≤n.
• For n ≥1, the only injective arrow in Hom∆(∆n−1, ∆n) whose image does not contain i is denoted δi
n.
Arrows of this form are called face morphisms, or simply faces.
• The only surjective arrow in Hom∆(∆n+1, ∆n) which maps both i and i+1 to i is denoted σi
n. Arrows
of this form are called degeneracy morphisms, or simply degeneracies.
• The object of sSet represented by ∆n is denoted ∆n as well, and is called the standard n-simplex.
• Given 0 ≤i ≤n, we refer to the corresponding morphism of simplicial sets ∆0 →∆n as the vertex i
of ∆n.
• We refer to the morphisms of simplicial sets δi
n : ∆n−1 →∆n for 0 ≤i ≤n as the faces of ∆n. We
refer to the morphism δi
n : ∆n−1 →∆n as the face opposite to the vertex i.
Let T be a simplicial set.
• The value of T at the object ∆n is denoted Tn.
• The elements of Tn are called n-simplices of T.
By lemma 2.1.4, they correspond to elements of
HomsSet(∆n, T). We will often abuse notation and identify these two sets.
• The 0-simplices of T are called the vertices of T. Given an n-simplex σ : ∆n →T, the vertex i of σ is
the 0-simplex of T obtained by restriction of σ along the morphism ∆0 →∆n induced by i.
• The map T(δi
n) : Tn →Tn−1 is denoted dn
i . Maps of this form are called face operators of T.
• The map T(σi
n) : Tn →Tn+1 is denoted sn
i . Maps of this form are called degeneracy operators of T.
• A simplex of T which is not in the image of a degeneracy operator is called nondegenerate.
We will in particular use the following simplicial sets.
• The boundary of ∆n is, informally, the simplicial set obtained from ∆n by removing the interior. It is
denoted by ∂∆n. Formally, it is the simplicial subset of ∆n defined as
(∂∆n)m = {φ : ∆m →∆n | φ is not surjective}.
We will later give another description of ∂∆n as the gluing of the faces of ∆n along their common faces
(proposition 6.2.5).
• The ith horn in ∆n is, informally, the simplicial set obtained from ∆n by removing the interior together
with the face opposite to the vertex i. It is denoted by Λn
i . Formally, it is the simplicial subset of ∆n
defined as
(Λn
i )m = {φ : ∆m →∆n | φ({0, 1, . . . , m}) ∪{i} ̸= {0, 1, . . . , n}}.
We will later give another description of Λn
i as the gluing of the faces of ∆n different from the one
opposite to the vertex i, along their common faces (proposition 7.1.3).
• The horn Λn
i is called an inner horn when 0 < i < n.
29


## Page 30


• The spine of ∆n is, informally, the simplicial subset of ∆n comprised of all vertices of ∆n together with
those edges that join successive vertices. Formally, it is the simplicial subset of ∆n whose k-simplices
are morphisms σ : ∆k →∆n satisfying σ(k) ≤σ(0) + 1. It is denoted Spine(∆n).
• Given two simplicial sets K and L, one defines the simplicial set HomsSet(K, L) by the formula
HomsSet(K, L)n = HomsSet(∆n × K, L),
with face and degeneracy operators determined by face and degeneracy maps between standard sim-
plices. Note that the set of 0-simplices of the latter is the set of morphisms of simplicial sets between
K and L.
• Let g : K →L be a morphism of simplicial sets and x a 0-simplex of L, which we regard as a morphism
of simplicial sets ∆0 →L. The fiber of g over x is the simplicial set defined as the pullback of the

We will also use the notion of semi-simplicial set, which we now introduce.
Notation 2.1.9. Let ∆+ denote the subcategory of ∆with the same objects, and morphisms corresponding
to injective maps only.
Remark 2.1.10. The category ∆+ can also be described as the subcategory of ∆with the same objects
and morphisms generated under composition by the face morphisms.
Definition 2.1.11. A semi-simplicial set is an object of the category of presheaves Pre(∆+). This category
is called the category of semi-simplicial sets and denoted sSet+.
Remark 2.1.12. To every simplicial set T is associated an underlying semi-simplicial set by restriction
along the functor (∆+)op →∆op. This semi-simplicial set sends ∆n to Tn for every n, and has structural
maps given by the faces operators of T and their compositions.
We now come back to the general setting of a (small) category C. We will see that every object of Pre(C)

Construction 2.1.19. The functor SingH is given by the formula
SingH(D)(C) = HomD(H(C), D).
This definition is made so that H and SingH are adjoint at the level of representable objects of Pre(C). But
since every object of Pre(C) is a colimit of representables, these are in fact adjoint functors. In particular,
the left Kan extension of H to Pre(C) commutes with colimits.

2.2
(∞, 1)-categories
This section is devoted to the theory of (∞, 1)-categories and follows [Lur09] and [Lur]. The theory of (∞, 1)-
categories is part of a more general theory of (∞, n)-categories, but (∞, 1)-categories are often referred to
as ∞-categories in the literature. We will do the same from now on.
The theory of ∞-categories can be regarded as a common generalization of ordinary category theory and
the classical homotopy theory of CW-complexes. The first two sections are dedicated to these, respectively.
The definition of an ∞-category is given in section 2.2.3, as well as the ∞-categorical counterparts of several
notions from classical category theory, such as compositions, functors and equivalences. Finally in section
2.2.4 we explain how topological categories provide a model for the theory of ∞-categories.
32


## Page 33


2.2.1
Ordinary categories as simplicial sets
To every (small) category C is associated a simplicial set N(C) called the nerve of C. The n-simplices of
N(C) are the commutative diagrams in C supported by the vertices and edges of the standard n-simplex.
From n = 0 to n = 3, these look as follows (the face and degeneracy operators of this simplicial set will be
described below).

For n = 2, the datum of such a diagram is actually equivalent to the datum of the two arrows f0,1 and f1,2,
since the arrow f0,2 is equal to the composition f1,2 ◦f0,1, because of the commutativity condition. More
generally, the datum of an n-simplex of N(C), with arrows fi,j : Xi →Xj for i ≤j, is equivalent to the
datum of the arrows fi,i+1 from i = 0 to i = n −1. Indeed, because of the commutativity condition, the
other arrows are obtained by the formula
fi,j = fj−1,j ◦. . . ◦fi,i+1.
The datum of an n-simplex of N(C) is therefore equivalent to the datum of a string of n composable
morphisms of C

The face and degeneracy operators of N(C) are described as follows.
Consider two integers n ≥0 and
0 ≤i ≤n. If 0 < i < n, the face operator dn
i corresponds to composing fi−1,1 with fi,i+1 and leaving the
other morphisms unchanged. If i = 0 or i = n, it corresponds to deleting fi and leaving the other morphisms
unchanged. The degeneracy operator sn
i corresponds to inserting the identity morphism of Xi in position i.
The nerve functor, defined from the category of small categories to the category of simplicial sets, is fully
faithful ([Lur, Proposition 1.3.3.1]). In other words, it allows to see ordinary categories as special kinds of
simplicial sets.
Given an arbitrary simplicial set S, we can think of its 0-simplices as objects, its 1-simplices as morphisms,
its 2-simplices as witnessing compositions of two morphisms and more generally, its n-simplices as some
kind of coherent compositions of n composable morphisms. Compositions in S may then not exist, and not
be unique in general. A simplicial set is isomorphic to the nerve of an ordinary category precisely when
compositions exist and are unique, in the sense that for every n ≥0, the map
HomsSet(∆n, S) →HomsSet(Spine(∆n), S)
is a bijection ([Lur, Corollary 1.5.7.9]). This condition is also equivalent to the condition that for every n ≥0
and every 0 < k < n, the map
HomsSet(∆n, S) →HomsSet(Λn
k, S)
33


## Page 34


is a bijection ([Lur, Proposition 1.3.4.1]).
An ∞-category is a simplicial set in which compositions exist and are unique up to a contractible space of
choices. We will give the precise definition in section 2.2.3. The next section is devoted to the classical
homotopy theory of spaces, both in terms of topological spaces and in terms of simplicial sets. It will in
particular allow to define precisely what we mean by a "space of choices".
2.2.2
The homotopy category of spaces
In this section, we discuss the classical homotopy category of spaces, first in terms of topological spaces and
then in terms of simplicial sets. We start with the following definition.
Definition 2.2.1. Let X, Y be two topological spaces and f : X →Y a continuous map. The map f is
called a weak homotopy equivalence when the following conditions are satisfied.
• The map π0(X) →π0(Y ) induced by f is a bijection.
• For every point x ∈X and every integer n ≥1, the morphism πn(X, x) →πn(Y, f(x)) induced by f is
an isomorphism.
This condition is in particular verified when f is a homotopy equivalence, i.e., when there exists a continuous
map g : Y →X such that f ◦g and g ◦f are homotopic to the identity. The condition that a map is a weak
homotopy equivalence is easier to check in practice.
Definition 2.2.2. The homotopy category of spaces is the localisation of the category of topological spaces
at the class of weak homotopy equivalences. It is denoted Spaces.
Denote by Top the category of topological spaces. The category Spaces satisfies the following universal
property: there exists a functor Top →Spaces such that for every category C, every functor F : Top →C
that sends weak homotopy equivalences to isomorphisms factors uniquely through Top →Spaces.
The category Spaces can be described in more concrete terms thanks to the following theorem. This theorem
can be deduced from the existence of the classical model structure on topological spaces first introduced by
Quillen in [Qui67, Chapter 2, Section 3].
Theorem 2.2.3. Denote by CW the full subcategory of Top whose objects are CW-complexes. Denote by
hCW the category described as follows.
• The objects of hCW are the CW-complexes.
• For every pair of CW-complexes (X, Y ), the set of morphisms of hCW between X and Y is the set of
homotopy classes of continuous maps between X and Y .
• The composition operation in hCW is induced from the composition operation in CW by passing to
homotopy classes.
Consider the composite of functors CW →Top →Spaces.
For every X, Y ∈CW, the induced map
HomCW(X, Y ) →HomSpaces(X, Y ) is compatible with the homotopy relation. The induced functor hCW →
Spaces is an equivalence of categories.
This generalizes a classical theorem of Whitehead asserting that a continuous map between CW-complexes
is a homotopy equivalence if and only if it is a weak homotopy equivalence.
It will be useful, while reading this paper, to have in mind the description of the category Spaces in terms
of simplicial sets. We now briefly present this description. We start with the notion of homotopy between
two morphisms of simplicial sets.
34


## Page 35


Definition 2.2.4. Let f, g : S →be two morphisms of simplicial sets. A homotopy between f and g is a
morphism of simplicial sets h : S × ∆1 →T such that hS×{0} = f and hS×{1} = g. If such a homotopy
exists, we say that f is homotopic to g.
Remark 2.2.5. Suppose given a homotopy h : S×∆1 →T from f to g. Passing to the geometric realization,
we get a morphism of topological spaces |h| : |S × ∆1| →|T|. Moreover there is a natural map of topological
spaces |S × ∆1| →|S| × |∆1| which is a homeomorphism ([Lur, Corollary 3.6.2.2]). The two maps |f| and
|g| are therefore homotopic.
The homotopy relation between morphisms of simplicial sets is not reflexive in general. For example, there
exists a homotopy between the morphisms ∆0
0−→∆1 and ∆0
1−→∆1 given by the identity morphism ∆1 →∆1,
but there does not exist a homotopy in the other direction. This relation is not transitive either in general.
For example, consider the three morphisms of simplicial sets ∆0
0,1,2
−−−→Λ2
1. The morphisms 0 and 1 are
homotopic, as well as the morphisms 1 and 2, but the morphisms 0 and 2 are not homotopic.
The failure of the homotopy relation between morphisms of simplicial sets to be an equivalence relation
disappears when the target is assumed to belong to some class of simplicial sets called Kan complexes which
we now introduce.
Definition 2.2.6. A Kan complex is a simplicial set K satisfying the following property: for any two integers
n and k such that n ≥0 and 0 ≤k ≤n, every morphism of the form Λn
k →K can be extended to a morphism
∆n →K. Here the simplicial set Λn
k is the kth horn in ∆n, introduced in 2.1.8.
Lemma 2.2.7. Let S be a simplicial set and K a Kan complex.
The homotopy relation on the set
HomsSet(S, K) is an equivalence relation.
Proof. See [Lur, Proposition 3.1.5.4].
Definition 2.2.8. The homotopy category of Kan complexes, denoted hKan, is the category defined as
follows.
• The objects of hKan are Kan complexes.
• Given two Kan complexes K and L, the set of morphisms of hKan between K and L is the set of
homotopy classes of morphisms of simplicial sets between K and L. Given f ∈HomsSet(K, L), we
denote by [f] its homotopy class.
• Given two composable morphisms in hKan represented by two morphisms of simplicial sets f and g,
their composition is defined by [f] ◦[g] = [f ◦g].
We know explain how the category hKan is related to the category Spaces. The comparison is done via the
pair of adjoint functors
| −| : sSet ⇄Top : Sing
introduced in section 2.1.
The geometric realization of every simplicial set has a CW structure ([Lur, Remark 1.2.3.12]). Moreover,
by remark 2.2.5, if two morphisms of Kan complexes are homotopic then their geometric realization are
homotopic as maps of topological spaces. In particular, the geometric realization functor induces a functor
h| −| : hKan →hCW ≃Spaces.
35


## Page 36


Consider on the other hand the singular simplicial set functor Sing : Top →sSet. For every topological
space X the simplicial set Sing(X) is a Kan complex ([Lur, Proposition 1.2.5.8]). Suppose we are given two
topological spaces X and Y and a homotopy h : X × [0, 1] →Y between two maps f and g. We get a map
of Kan complexes Sing(X × [0, 1]) →Sing(Y ). We want to deduce from this the existence of a homotopy
between the maps Sing(f) and Sing(g). To this end note that since Sing is a right adjoint it commutes
with products, and therefore the natural map Sing(X × [0, 1]) →Sing(X) × Sing([0, 1]) is an isomorphism
of simplicial sets. Furthermore by definition of the geometric realization functor one has

and consequently one has a homeomorphism defined by

By adjunction this corresponds to a morphism of simplicial set ∆1 →Sing([0, 1]). Now the composite
Sing(X) × ∆1 →Sing(X) × Sing([0, 1])

is a homotopy between Sing(X) and Sing(Y ). In particular the functor Sing induces a functor
hSing : hCW →hKan.
We have the following lemma.
Lemma 2.2.9. The adjunction between the functors |−| and Sing induces an adjunction between the functors
h| −| and hSing.
Proof. By adjunction, for every topological space X and every simplicial set S there is a natural identification
HomTop(|S|, X) = HomsSet(S, Sing(X)).
What we want to show is equivalent to the condition that, when S is a Kan complex and X is a CW-complex,
this identification is compatible with the homotopy relation on both sides. We prove that this actually holds
without these restrictions on S and X. Indeed, identifying [0, 1] and |∆1| as above we have
HomTop(|S| × [0, 1], X) = HomTop(|S| × |∆1|, X)
= HomTop(|S × ∆1|, X)
= HomsSet(S × ∆1, Sing(X))
and therefore the homotopy relation on HomTop(|S|, X) coincides with the homotopy relation on HomsSet(S, Sing(X)).
Theorem 2.2.10. The functor h| −| is an equivalence of categories.
Proof. A proof is given in [Lur, Section 3.6].
Remark 2.2.11. Recall the following general fact from category theory. If we are given a functor F : C →D
that admits a right adjoint G then the following conditions are equivalent.
36


## Page 37


(i) The functor F is an equivalence.
(ii) The functor G is an equivalence.
(iii) The unit of the adjunction IdC →G ◦F is a natural equivalence.
(iv) The counit of the adjunction F ◦G →IdD is a natural equivalence.
In particular, theorem 2.2.10 is equivalent to each of the following assertions.
(i) The functor hSing is an equivalence of categories.
(ii) For every Kan complex K the morphism of Kan complexes K →Sing(|K|) is a homotopy equivalence.
(iii) For every CW-complex X the map of topological spaces |Sing(X)| →X is a homotopy equivalence.
Remark 2.2.12. Actually a stronger version of theorem 2.2.10 holds: for every topological space X the
natural map X →|Sing(X)| is a weak homotopy equivalence. This is due to Milnor in [Mil57] and a proof
is also given in [Lur, Theorem 3.6.4.1]. This formally implies that for every topological spaces X, Y , a map
f : X →Y is a weak homotopy equivalence if and only if the morphism of Kan complexes Sing(f) is a
homotopy equivalence. Indeed, there is a commutative diagram of topological spaces
|Sing(X)|

and since the vertical maps are weak homotopy equivalences, the condition that f is a weak homotopy

|Sing(Y )| admit CW structures, this is equivalent to the condition that |Sing(f)| is a homotopy equivalence
and therefore, by theorem 2.2.10, to the condition that Sing(f) is a homotopy equivalence.
2.2.3
∞-categories: definitions
The goal of this section is to define ∞-categories and explain how several concepts from ordinary category
theory generalize to this setting.
We begin with two definitions.
Definition 2.2.13. Let C be a category. A lifting problem in C is a commutative solid arrow diagram of the

The definition of an ∞-category is the following.
Definition 2.2.15. An ∞-category is a simplicial set C that satisfies the following equivalent conditions.
(i) For any two integers n and k such that n ≥0 and 0 < k < n, every lifting problem of the form

The equivalence between conditions (i) and (ii) is due to Joyal and a proof can be found in [Lur, Theorem
1.5.6.1]. Note that condition (iii) reduces to condition (ii) in the case n = 2. Conversely a proof of the
implication (i) ⇒(iii) is given in [Lur, Remark 1.5.7.7].
Example 2.2.16. By the discussion of section 2.2.1, the nerve of every (small) category is an ∞-category.
Example 2.2.17. Every Kan complex is an ∞-category, since it satisfies condition (i) for any two integers
n ≥0 and 0 ≤k ≤n.
Condition (i) is called the inner horn filling property and is in practice the easiest to check. On the other hand,
condition (ii) justifies the idea that the compositions in an ∞-category are well-defined up to a contractible
space of choices, as we now explain. Let C be an ∞-category.
Definition 2.2.18. The objects of C are the 0-simplices of C. The morphisms of C are the 1-simplices of C.
Given a morphism γ of C, the source of γ is the object x = d1
1(γ) and the target of γ is the object y = d1
0(γ).
The morphism γ is called a morphism from x to y.

Note that when C is the nerve of an ordinary category, this definition coincides with the usual one.
Compositions in an ∞-category are defined as follows.
Definition 2.2.19. Suppose given two morphisms f, g of C such that the target of f is equal to the source
of g. A morphism h is called a composition of f and g if there exists a 2-simplex σ of C such that d2
0(σ) = g,

and g. Something stronger actually holds: a 2-simplex σ as in definition 2.2.19 is the same as a 0-simplex of
the fiber (in the sense of notation and terminology 2.1.8) of the morphism

1, C)
over the horn defined by f and g. This fiber can be regarded as parametrizing the compositions between
f and g and by condition (ii) in definition 2.2.15, this is a contractible Kan complex. In other words, the
compositions of f and g are parametrized by a contractible space.
Definition 2.2.20. For an object X ∈C, the morphism s0
0(X) is called the identity morphism of X.
Definition 2.2.21. A morphism f from X to Y is called an isomorphism if there exists a morphism g from
Y to X such that the identity morphism of X is a composition of f and g and the identity morphism of
Y is composition of g and f. If there exists an isomorphism between X and Y , we say that X and Y are
isomorphic.
The notion of functor of categories generalizes to ∞-categories as follows.
Definition 2.2.22. A functor between ∞-categories is a morphism between the corresponding simplicial
sets.
Proposition 2.2.23. If K is a simplicial set and C an ∞-category, the simplicial set Hom(K, D) is an
∞-category.
Proof. See [Lur09, Proposition 1.2.7.3].
The notion of equivalence of categories generalizes to ∞-categories as follows.
Definition 2.2.24. A functor F between two ∞-categories C and D is called an equivalence if there exists
a functor G between D and C such that F ◦G and G ◦F are isomorphic to the identity as objects of the
∞-categories Hom(D, D) and Hom(C, C) respectively.
It is often convenient to prove that a functor between ordinary categories is an equivalence by verifying that
it is fully faithful and essentially surjective. This characterization of equivalences extends to ∞-categories
as we now discuss.
39


## Page 40


Definition 2.2.25. A functor of ∞-categories F : C →D is called essentially surjective if for every object
y of D, there exists an object x of C such that F(x) and y are isomorphic.
To define full faithfulness we need the following definition.
Definition 2.2.26. Let C be a simplicial set and x, y two 0-simplices of C. We define the simplicial set
HomC(x, y) to be the fiber over (x, y) of the morphism
HomsSet(∆1, C)
d1

−−−−→C × C.
When C is an ∞-category, we refer to this simplicial set as the space of morphisms of C between x and y.
Remark 2.2.27. This terminology comes from the fact that HomC(x, y) is a Kan complex as soon as C is
an ∞-category ([Lur, Proposition 4.6.1.10]).
Definition 2.2.28. A functor of ∞-categories F : C →D is called fully faithful if for every pair of objects
(x, y) of C, the morphism of Kan complexes induced by F
HomC(x, y) →HomD(F(x), F(y))
is a homotopy equivalence.
We then have the following theorem.
Theorem 2.2.29. A functor between ∞-categories is an equivalence if and only if it is fully faithful and
essentially surjective.
Proof. See [Lur, Theorem 4.6.2.21].
Remark 2.2.30. To every ∞-category C is associated an ordinary category hC called the homotopy category
of C. Its objects are the objects of C and given two objects x, y of C, the set of morphism of hC between
these is the set π0(HomD(x, y)), which denotes the set of homotopy classes of maps ∆0 →HomD(x, y) (this
set is well-defined since HomD(x, y) is a Kan complex). The isomorphisms of C are precisely those that yield
isomorphisms in the homotopy category. For a discussion of the homotopy category construction, we refer
to [Lur, Section 1.4.5].
Until now we have defined in this section the notion of ∞-category, functor between ∞-categories and
equivalence of ∞-categories. The ∞-categories are actually the objects of a category in which equivalences
become isomorphisms.
Definition 2.2.31. The category of ∞-categories, denoted ∞Cat, is the category described as follows.
• The objects of ∞Cat are the ∞-categories.
• Given two ∞-categories C and D, the set of morphisms of ∞Cat between C and D is the set of functors
of ∞-categories between C and D modded out by the relation of being naturally equivalent, meaning
isomorphic as objects of the ∞-category Hom(C, D) (this is an equivalence relation according to remark
2.2.30).
Remark 2.2.32. Denote by sSet∞Cat the full subcategory of sSet whose objects are ∞-categories. Note that
the natural functor sSet∞Cat →∞Cat sends equivalences of ∞-categories to isomorphisms. By considering
the Joyal model structure on sSet, one can prove that this functor exhibits ∞Cat as the localization of
sSet∞Cat with respect to the class of equivalences of ∞-categories. For a discussion of the Joyal model
structure, we refer the reader to [Lur09, Section 2.2.5].
40


## Page 41


Remark 2.2.33. In definition 2.2.15 we defined an ∞-category to be a simplicial set satisfying certain
conditions. However, as definition 2.2.31 suggests, two ∞-categories should be considered "the same" not
only when they are isomorphic as simplicial sets, but more generally when they are equivalent, i.e., isomorphic
as objects of ∞Cat. Strictly speaking, simplicial sets satisfying the equivalent conditions of definition 2.2.15
should therefore be regarded as models for ∞-categories. Nevertheless, throughout this paper we will refer
to them simply as "∞-categories".
2.2.4
Topological categories and ∞-categories
We saw in the previous section that for every two objects x, y of an ∞-category, there is a Kan complex of
morphisms between them. Moreover, by the discussion of section 2.2.2, the category of Kan complexes can be
used to describe the homotopy category of spaces. This leads to a description of the theory of ∞-categories
in terms of topological categories instead of simplicial sets, which we briefly review in this section.
Definition 2.2.34. A topological category is a category enriched over the category of compactly generated
weakly Hausdorff topological spaces. We denote the category of topological categories by CatTop.
A topological category is thus a category such that the set of morphisms between any two given objects has a
structure of compactly generated weakly Hausdorff topological space, and for every triple of objects X, Y, Z,
the composition map Hom(X, Y ) × Hom(Y, Z) →Hom(X, Z) is continuous. A functor between topological
categories is a functor such that the associated maps between morphism topological spaces are continuous.
Remark 2.2.35. The product Hom(X, Y ) × Hom(Y, Z) above has to be taken in the category of compactly
generated topological spaces. It has the same underlying set as the usual product in the category of topo-
logical spaces, but not the same topology in general. The reason why we work with compactly generated
topological spaces is because we need the geometric realization functor
| −| : sSet →Topological spaces
to commutes with products of simplicial sets and of topological spaces, in order to define the geometric
realization of a simplicial category (in section 4.1). Apart from this technical point, this subtlety can be
ignored.
In order to explain how topological categories are related to ∞-categories, we will need an appropriate notion
of isomorphism in a topological category, which is weaker than the usual one. In order to introduce it, we
first need the following definition.
Definition 2.2.36. Let C be a topological category, X, Y two objects of C and f, g ∈HomC (X, Y ). A
homotopy between f and g is a continuous map h : [0, 1] →HomC (X, Y ) such that h(0) = f and h(1) = g.
If such a homotopy exists, we say that f and g are homotopic.
Definition 2.2.37. Let C be a topological category and f : X →Y a morphism in C. The morphism f is
called an isomorphism when there exists a morphism g : Y →X such that f ◦g is homotopic to the identity
of Y and g ◦f is homotopic to the identity of X.
In order to compare the theory of topological categories and the theory of ∞-categories, the appropriate
notion of equivalence between topological categories is the following (compare with theorem 2.2.29).
Definition 2.2.38. A functor between topological categories F : C →D is called an equivalence when it
satisfies the following two conditions.
• Essential surjectivity: for every object Y ∈D, there exists an object X ∈C such that F(X) and Y
are isomorphic (in the sense of definition 2.2.37).
41


## Page 42


• Full faithfulness: For every pair of objects (X, Y ) of C , the map of topological spaces F(X, Y ) :
HomC (X, Y ) →HomD(F(X), F(Y )) is a weak homotopy equivalence.
Contrary to the notion of equivalence of ∞-categories introduced in definition 2.2.24, an equivalence of topo-
logical categories does not always admit an inverse, even up to homotopy. Two topological categories should
therefore be considered equivalent when they are connected by a zigzag of equivalences. More generally, one
can consider the localization of the category of topological categories at the class of equivalences. It turns
out that the resulting category is equivalent to ∞Cat, as we now explain.
The category of topological categories is related to the category of simplicial sets by mean of a functor called
the homotopy coherent nerve functor
N : CatTop →sSet.
We will study this functor in detail in section 4. We have the following theorem.
Theorem 2.2.39. The functor N sends every topological category to an ∞-category, and sends equivalences
between topological categories to equivalences between ∞-categories. The induced functor CatTop →∞Cat
exhibits ∞Cat as the localization of CatTop with respect to the class of equivalences.
Proof. A proof if given in [Lur09, Theorem 1.1.5.13]. Another proof is given in [Joy07].
Let C be a topological category. On the one hand, for every pair of objects (X, Y ) of C we have a topological
space of morphisms HomC (X, Y ) and therefore a Kan complex Sing(HomC (X, Y )). On the other hand,
according to theorem 2.2.39, there is an ∞-category N(C ) associated to C. We thus have, for every pair
of objects (x, y) of N(C ), the Kan complex HomN (C )(x, y) defined in 2.2.26. We can wonder if these two
notions of morphism spaces coincides. This is indeed the case by virtue of the following proposition.
Proposition 2.2.40. There is a natural bijection between the sets of objects of the topological category C and
the ∞-category N(C ). Moreover, for every pair of objects (X, Y ) of C, the Kan complexes Sing(HomC (X, Y ))
and HomN(C )(X, Y ) are naturally homotopy equivalent.
Proof. The natural bijection between the sets of objects follows from the construction of the functor N (see
section 4). The natural homotopy equivalence of morphism spaces is discussed in [Lur09, Section 2.2], we
will come back to it in section 7.2 (see in particular remark 7.2.11).
Remark 2.2.41. Given a topological category C , there are two associated construction of a "homotopy
category". One can either consider the category π0(C) with the same set of objects as C and Homπ0(C )(x, y) =
π0(HomC (x, y)) for every pair of objects x, y, or one can consider the homotopy category of the ∞-category
N(C ). By proposition 2.2.40 these two categories are naturally isomorphic. A proof of this in particular can
be found in ([Lur, Proposition 2.4.6.9]).
2.3
Stratified topological spaces, exit paths and stratified simplicial sets
This section is devoted to the notion of stratification and its homotopy-theoretic study. In section 2.3.1
we introduce the notions of stratified topological space and exit path. In section 2.3.2 we define conically
stratified spaces, we introduce the exit path ∞-category associated with a conically stratified space, and we
give a description of the morphism spaces of this ∞-category in terms of spaces of exit paths. In section 2.3.3
we introduce the analogues of stratified topological spaces in the category of simplicial sets, called stratified
simplicial sets. Finally, in section 2.3.4, we introduce cylindrically stratified topological spaces and we give
for these an alternative description of the spaces of exit paths, up to homotopy.
42

2.3.1
Stratified topological spaces and exit paths
Remark 2.3.1. The notion of stratified topological space that we review in this section is that of a poset-
stratified space. Let us mention that the particular stratification that interests us in this paper falls into the
more restricted realm of Whitney stratified spaces, which are defined by extra geometric conditions. This
will be the subject of section 3 (see in particular 3.2.1 for the definition). On the other hand, the framework
of poset-stratified spaces is the most general one and has the advantage of being well-suited for the study of
stratified spaces from a homotopy theoretic perspective, as it permits the development of a parallel theory
of stratified simplicial sets, which we will review in section 2.3.3.
For an overview of the various notions of stratified topological spaces and relations between them, see
[WWY24].
Let A be a poset.
Definition 2.3.2. Given a ∈A, we let A≥a (resp. A>a, A≤a, A<a) denote the sub-poset of A consisting of
those elements b ∈A such that b ≥a (resp. b > a, b ≤a, b < a).
Definition 2.3.3. The Alexandrov topology on A is the topology generated by the open subsets A≥a.
Remark 2.3.4. A subset U ⊆A is open for the Alexandrov topology if and only if it is closed upwards,
meaning a ∈U and b ≥a implies b ∈U.
Definition 2.3.5. Let Y be a topological space. An A-stratification on Y is a continuous map π : Y →A,
where A is endowed with the Alexandrov topology. For every a ∈A, we denote Ya = π−1({a}) and refer
to it as the a-stratum of Y . We will often abuse notation and denote the A-stratified space π : Y →A by
(Y, A) without specifying the stratifying map.
Remark 2.3.6. Said differently, a stratification of Y is a decomposition of Y as a disjoint union of strata
Y =
G
a∈A
Ya
such that for every a ∈A, the union of the strata Yb for b ≥a is open in Y .
Example 2.3.7. Every (0 < 1)-stratification of Y determines a closed subset Y0 ⊆Y and conversely, every
closed subset F ⊆Y determines a (0 < 1)-stratification of Y by the conditions that Y0 = F and Y1 = Y \F.
This defines a bijection between the set of (0 < 1)-stratifications of Y , and the set of closed subsets of Y .
Notation 2.3.8. We denote by TopA the overcategory Top/A.
The category TopA is thus the category whose objects are the A-stratified topological spaces and whose
morphisms are the stratum-preserving continuous maps between these.
Proposition 2.3.9. The category TopA admits all small limits and small colimits. Moreover, the forgetful
functor TopA →Top commutes with colimits.
Proof. To prove that TopA admits all small limits, we prove that it admits all equalizers and all small
products. To prove that it admits all equalizers, consider two arrows in TopA

## Page 44


The two arrows f and g admit an equalizer in Top, given by the subset {x ∈X | f(x) = g(x)} ⊆X endowed
with the topology induced by the topology of X. This equalizer inherits an A-stratification from the one of
X. The resulting A-stratified space is an equalizer of the above two arrows in TopA.
To prove that TopA admits all small products, consider a family of A-stratified spaces (Xj, A)j∈J for some
set J. The fiber product in Top of the family of topological spaces (Xj)j∈J over A is naturally endowed with
an A-stratification. The resulting A-stratified space is the product of the family (Xj, A)j∈J in TopA.
To prove that TopA admits all small colimits, let us consider a small category D and a functor G : D →TopA.
It induces a functor D →Top by forgetting the stratifications. This functor admits a colimit X ∈Top, which
is endowed with a natural map X →A. The latter is an A-stratification of X which is a colimit of G. This
also shows that the forgetful functor TopA →Top commutes with colimits.
Let us fix an A-stratification of Y . We denote by I the interval [0, 1].
Definition 2.3.10. An exit path in Y is a path γ : I →Y such that there exist a, b ∈A with π(γ(0)) = a
and π(γ(t)) = b for all t > 0 (note that this implies a ≤b).
See figure 4 for an illustration of this definition.
Remark 2.3.11. Note that in the statement of definition 2.3.10, one can have a = b. In other words, a path
in Y that stays in the same stratum at all times, is an exit path.
The purpose of the next section is to introduce a notion of composition between exit paths.
