The definition of a conically stratified space is the following.
Definition 2.3.26. Let A be a poset, let Y be an A-stratified space and let y be a point of Y . We let a
be the point of A such that y ∈Ya. We say that Y is conically stratified at the point y if there exists a
A>a-stratified space l, a topological space V , and an open embedding C(l) × V ,→Y of A-stratified spaces
whose image contains y. Here, we regard C(l) as endowed with an A≥a stratification by identifying A◁
>a
with A≥a, and we regard C(l) × V as endowed with the A≥a-stratification defined as the composition of the
A≥a-stratification on C(l) with the projection C(l) × V →C(l). Such an open embedding is called a local
cone-like description of the stratification near y.
We say that Y is conically stratified if it is conically stratified at every point y ∈Y .

2.3.3
Stratified simplicial sets
Suppose we have an A-stratified space Y and an n-simplex σ of SingA(Y ). This comes with a continuous
map |∆n| →Y . Recall that |∆n| is the convex hull of the canonical basis (e0, . . . , en) of Rn+1. For every
integer 0 ≤i ≤n, let us define the element ai ∈A by the condition that σ(ei) ∈Yai. For every integer
0 ≤i < n, there is a corresponding morphism ∆1 →∆n carrying 0 to i and 1 to i + 1. This allows to
associate to σ and i a 1-simplex of SingA(Y ), and the latter is an exit path from σ(ei) to σ(ei+1); we deduce
that ai ≤ai+1. We have thus a finite increasing sequence [a0 ≤. . . ≤an] of elements of A. This observation
leads to an analogue of the notion of stratified topological space, in the category of simplicial sets, which we
now introduce.
Definition 2.3.31. The nerve of A, denoted N(A), is the nerve of the following category:
ß Objects = Elements of A,
Hom(a, b) = ∗if a ≤b and Hom(a, b) = ∅otherwise.
Remark 2.3.32. We can alternatively regard the finite increasing sequences of elements of A of length n as
the n-simplices of the nerve of A.
Assigning to a simplex of SingA(Y ) the corresponding finite increasing sequence of elements of A (as explained
at the beginning of this section) determines a morphism of simplicial sets from SingA(Y ) to the nerve of A.
This is a particular case of the following definition.
Definition 2.3.33. The category of simplicial sets stratified by A is the overcategory sSet/N(A). It is denoted
sSetA.

Let Y be an A-stratified topological space and x, y two points of Y . We finish this section by studying
the simplicial set HomSingA(X)(x, y) (definition 2.2.26). Before stating the result, we introduce the following
definition.
Definition 2.3.44.
• The space of exit paths in (Y, A) between x and y is the set of exit paths in (Y, A)
starting at x and ending at y, endowed with the compact-open topology. It is denoted Exit(x, y).
• Given two elements a, b ∈A, the space of exit paths in (Y, A) between Ya and Yb is the set of exit paths
in (Y, A) starting in Ya and ending in Yb, endowed with the compact-open topology. It is denoted
Exit(Ya, Yb).
Proposition 2.3.45. There is a natural identification of simplicial sets
HomSingA(X)(x, y) = Sing(Exit(x, y)).

## Page 51


In particular, when the stratification of Y by A is conical, the exit path ∞-category of (Y, A) is an ∞-category
whose points are the points of Y , and whose spaces of morphisms are the spaces of exit paths between the
corresponding points.
Proof of proposition 2.3.45. Denote by a, b the respective images of x, y in A. Assume that HomSingA(X)(x, y)
is nonempty. Then x and y are connected by an exit path, so a ≤b. Let us endow ∆1 and |∆1| with the
corresponding stratifications by A. For every integer n ≥0 we also endow ∆1 × ∆n and |∆1| × |∆n| with
the stratifications induced by the first projection. Consider an n-simplex of HomSingA(X)(x, y)
σ : ∆1 × ∆n →SingA(Y ).
The map σ is compatible with the arrows to N(A) on both sides. Indeed, by remark 2.3.37 it suffices to
verify this compatibility on vertices, where it follows from the fact that σ is constant equal to x in restriction
to 0 × ∆n and equal to y in restriction to 1 × ∆n.
By adjunction we have
HomsSetA(∆1 × ∆n, SingA(Y )) = HomTopA(|∆1 × ∆n|, Y )
= HomTopA((|∆1| × |∆n|, Y )
= HomTop(|∆n|, HomTopA(|∆|1, Y )),
where HomTopA denotes the subset of stratified maps endowed with the topology induced by the compact-
open topology.
The above identification restricts to an identification of sets
HomSingA(X)(x, y)n = HomTop(|∆n|, Exit(x, y)).
These identifications are functorial in n, so that we finally get and identification of simplicial sets
HomSingA(X)(x, y) = Sing(Exit(x, y)).
Remark 2.3.46. By theorem 2.3.29 one can associate to every conically stratified topological space (Y, A)
an ordinary category defined to be the homotopy category of the ∞-category SingA(Y ). The objects of this
category are the points of X and, by proposition 2.3.45, the set of morphisms between every pair of points
(x, y) is the set π0(Exit(x, y)). The composition law is defined as in figure 21; it is in particular a consequence
of theorem 2.3.29 that this composition law is well-defined.
We close this section with the following proposition, which we will be useful to us later.
Proposition 2.3.47. Let Y be a metrizable A-stratified space. Then the following two conditions are equiv-
alent:
(i) the simplicial set SingA(Y ) is an ∞-category,
(ii) for every pair of elements a < b of A, the starting point evaluation map Exit(Ya, Yb) →Ya is a Serre
fibration.
Proof. This a particular case of [Waa24, Proposition 5.1.4]. Note that, in that paper, "categorically fibrant"
is equivalent to condition (i) (see [Waa24, Definition 5.1.2]).

2.3.4
A geometric description of the spaces of exit paths in cylindrical stratifications
Since the exit path ∞-category of a conically stratified space has spaces of exit paths as spaces of morphisms
(proposition 2.3.45), we are interested in spaces of exit paths up to weak homotopy equivalence. The main
result of this section is proposition 2.3.57, which provides an alternative description of the space of exit paths
between two strata, up to weak homotopy equivalence, in what is called a cylindrical stratification. This is
based on [MW24, Construction 2.41], and we give details for arguments that are only sketched there.
This section is only concerned with stratified topological spaces with two strata, or equivalently, with topo-
logical spaces stratified by the poset 0 < 1. Recall from example 2.3.7 that such a datum is equivalent to
the datum of a topological space and a closed subset.
We will work with the following definition of mapping cylinder.
Definition 2.3.48. Let r : L →Y be a continuous map between topological spaces. The mapping cylinder
of r, denoted Cyl(r), is the set Y ∪L × (0, 1] endowed with the minimal topology such that the following
two conditions hold:
(i) the inclusion L × (0, 1] →Cyl(r) is an open embedding,
(ii) the map R : Cyl(r) →Y × [0, 1] obtained as the union of the two maps
Y

This topology can be explicitly described as follows: a subset U ⊂Cyl(r) is open if and only if U ∩L×(0, 1]
is open and, for every y ∈Y ∩U, U contains a set of the form V ∪r−1(V ) × (0, ε) for some neighborhood V

2.4
Morse-Smale pairs and flow categories
The goal of this subsection is to present the definitions and results of Morse theory that we are going to
use. We introduce the notions of Morse function, Morse-Smale pair, and prove that the stable manifolds of
a Morse-Smale pair form a stratification in the sense of definition 2.3.5 (proposition 2.4.26). We then define
the flow category of a Morse-Smale pair and discuss smoothness of its morphism spaces. As an important
consequence, we obtain that the space of morphisms between two critical points is homotopy equivalent to
the space of unbroken pseudo-gradient trajectories connecting them (corollary 2.4.42).
A general reference for the unproven results of this section is the first part of the book [AD14].
We let X be a smooth manifold of dimension n.
2.4.1
Morse-Smale pairs and unbroken trajectories
Definition 2.4.1. A Morse function on X is a smooth function f : X →R whose critical points are
nondegenerate, meaning that for any critical point a of f, the Hessian of f at a is a nondegenerate symmetric
bilinear 2−form on the tangent space of X at a.
Morse functions always exist on X, and they form an open dense subset of C∞(X, R) for the C∞topology
([AD14, Theorem 1.2.5]).
The following lemma is known as the Morse lemma ([AD14, Theorem 1.3.1]).
Lemma 2.4.2. Let a ∈X be a nondegenerate critical point of a real smooth function. There exist local
coordinates (x1, . . . , xn) at a in which this function is written as follows
f(x) = f(a) −

Definition 2.4.3. Let a ∈X be a critical point of a real smooth function. A local chart at a with coordinates
as in lemma 2.4.2 is called a Morse chart for this function at a.
58


## Page 59


Definition 2.4.4. The integer 0 ≤k ≤n from the expression in lemma 2.4.2 is the maximal dimension of
a negative definite subspace for the Hessian of f at a, and therefore does not depend on the Morse chart at
a. It is called the Morse index, or simply the index, of the critical point a with respect to the function, and
is denoted by |a|.
Definition 2.4.5. Let f : X →R be a Morse function. A negative pseudo-gradient adapted to f is a vector
field ξ on X satisfying the following conditions:
• ξ(x) = 0 if and only if x is a critical point of f, and if not, dfx(ξ(x)) < 0.
• For every critical point a, there exists a Morse chart for f at a in which ξ coincides with the Euclidean
negative gradient of f. In other words, ξ is written in this chart as follows

A negative pseudo-gradient adapted to f always exists ([AD14, Theorem 2.2.5]).
Assume from now on that X is compact without boundary, and fix a negative pseudo-gradient ξ adapted to
f. The flow of ξ is then defined for all times t ∈R.
Notation 2.4.6. We denote by ϕt(x) the flow of ξ at time t, starting from the point x ∈X.
Definition 2.4.7. A flow line of ξ is a map of the form
(p, q)

Proposition 2.4.8. The limits at −∞and +∞of any flow line of ξ defined on (−∞, +∞) exist and are
critical points of f.
Proof. See [AD14, Theorem 2.1.6].
Definition 2.4.9. For every critical point a of f we define the stable manifold of a as
W s(a) = {x ∈X | ϕt(x) −→
t→+∞a},
and the unstable manifold of a as
W u(a) = {x ∈X | ϕt(x) −→
t→−∞a}.
Example 2.4.10. Figure 3 provides examples of stable manifolds.
Proposition 2.4.11. The stable and unstable manifolds of every critical point are smooth submanifolds of
X, diffeomorphic to Rn−|a| and R|a|, respectively.
Proof. See [AD14, Section 2.1.d].
Remark 2.4.12. Given a critical point a of f, one can alternatively think of the points of W s(a) (resp.
W u(a)) as (unbroken) trajectories of ξ ending at a (resp. starting from a).
59


## Page 60


Definition 2.4.13. For any two critical points a and b, the quotient space of W u(a)∩W s(b) by the action of
R induced by the flow of ξ is called the space of unbroken trajectories of ξ connecting a to b, and is denoted
◦
M(a, b) 7. An element of this quotient is called an unbroken trajectory of ξ connecting a to b.
Suppose that there exists an unbroken trajectory γ ∈
◦
M(a, b), and take a representing element x ∈W u(a) ∩
W s(b) for it. The orbit of x under the flow of ξ is the image of R by the map t 7→ϕt(x). This map tends to
a as t tends to −∞, and tends to b as t tends to +∞.
Definition 2.4.14. The image of γ is the union of {a, b} with the orbit of x under the flow of ξ.
Note that this definition does not depend on the choice of the representing element x.
Remark 2.4.15. Every critical point is connected to itself by the constant trajectory of ξ at that point.
Definition 2.4.16. We say that ξ satisfies Smale’s transversality condition if for every two distinct critical
points a and b, the submanifolds W u(a) and W s(b) intersect transversely.
Remark 2.4.17. When ξ satisfies Smale’s transversality condition, the intersection W u(a) ∩W s(b) is a
submanifold of X of dimension |a| −|b|. Moreover, when f(a) > f(b), the space
◦
M(a, b) (definition 2.4.13)
is naturally identified with the transverse intersection of W u(a) ∩W s(b) with any regular level set of f of
level strictly comprised between f(a) and f(b). It is endowed in this way with the structure of a smooth
manifold of dimension |a| −|b| −1.
Remark 2.4.18. By remark 2.4.17, if ξ satisfies Smale’s transversality condition and a, b are two critical
points of f such that there exists a non constant trajectory of ξ starting at a and ending at b, then |a| > |b|.
Definition 2.4.19. The datum (f, ξ) of a Morse function on X and of a negative pseudo-gradient adapted
to f satisfying Smale’s transversality condition is called a Morse-Smale pair on X.
Let us fix a Morse-Smale pair (f, ξ) on X and denote by A the set of critical points of f.
Proposition 2.4.20. The relation ≤on A defined as: a ≤b if and only if
◦
M(a, b) is not empty, is a partial
order.
Proof. This relation is reflexive by remark 2.4.15. It is antisymmetric because, if a and b are two critical
point such that a is connected to b by an unbroken trajectory of ξ and b is connected to a by an unbroken
trajectory of ξ, then, since the values of f are strictly decreasing along non-constant flow lines of ξ, we must
have that these two trajectories are constant, and therefore that a = b. Transitivity is more delicate and
follows from proposition 2.4.41 below.
Definition 2.4.21. The partial order ≤on A from proposition 2.4.20 is called the Smale ordering.
Remark 2.4.22. We will see below equivalent characterizations of this partial order relation (corollary
3.3.6).

2.4.2
The stratification by the stable manifolds of a Morse-Smale pair
Assume X is closed and let (f, ξ) be a Morse-Smale pair on X. The goal of this section is to introduce the
stratification associated with this data, that we will study throughout the paper.
Proposition 2.4.26. Recall the notation ϕ for the flow of ξ (notation 2.4.6). The map
π :
X
−→

7−→

t→+∞ϕt(x),
which is well-defined by proposition 2.4.8, is a stratification of X by A (in the sense of definition 2.3.5),
satisfying Xa = W s(a) for all a ∈A.
We refer to this stratification as the stratification by the stable
manifolds of ξ.
Example 2.4.27. Example 1.1.3 illustrates this stratification for a specific example of (X, f, ξ).
Proof. Recall that we denote by A≥a the open subset of A formed by those b ∈A such that b ≥a. We have
to prove that for every a ∈A, π−1(A≥a) is an open subset of X, that is, for every x ∈X such that π(x) ≥a
there exists a neighborhood U of x in X such that for every y ∈U, π(y) ≥a.
We first argue that it suffices to treat the case where x is a critical point. Assume indeed that the property
holds for the critical point π(x). It means that there exists a neighborhood V of π(x) such that for every
61

A first basic observation, connecting the discussion in this section with that in section 2.3, is that to every
unbroken trajectory of ξ there corresponds an exit path for the stratification of X by stable manifolds. To
make this precise, we make the following construction.
Construction 2.4.29. Let a, b by two distinct critical points of f and suppose that there exists an unbroken
trajectory γ of ξ connecting a to b. Let eγ ⊂X be the image of γ (in the sense of definition 2.4.14). We
endow eγ with the topology induced by that on X. The restriction of f to eγ induces a homeomorphism

By definition, it satisfies f(γ(s)) = s for every s ∈[f(b), f(a)]. We refer to this map as the parametrization
of γ by the values of f. Further, by precomposing this map by the affine nonincreasing surjection [0, 1] →
[f(b), f(a)] 8, we obtain a path denoted
ιa,b(γ) : [0, 1] →X,
which we refer to as the path in X associated with γ. These constructions extend to the case a = b in a
straightforward way.
Lemma 2.4.30. Let a, b be two critical points of f and assume that there exists an unbroken trajectory γ of
ξ connecting a to b. Then the path in X associated with γ (construction 2.4.29) is an exit path with respect
to the stratification of X by the stable manifolds of ξ.
Proof. In the case where a = b, ιa,b(γ) is constant and is therefore an exit path. Let us assume that a ̸= b.
We have ιa,b(γ)(0) = a ∈Xa and ιa,b(γ)(1) = b ∈Xb. For every s ∈]0, 1[, ιa,b(γ)(s) belongs to the image
of a flow line of ξ defined on (−∞, +∞) whose limit at +∞is b, hence ιa,b(γ)(s) ∈Xb. This proves that
ιa,b(γ) is an exit path.
Remark 2.4.31. By lemma 2.4.30, we have, for every pair of critical points a, b of f, a continuous inclusion
ιa,b :
◦

We will study this inclusion in section 3, where we will prove that it is a weak homotopy equivalence.
Remark 2.4.32. In the case when a < b, the map ιa,b is defined by using the affine decreasing bijection
[0, 1] →[f(b), f(a)], but we could have defined it using any decreasing homeomorphism between [0, 1] and
[f(b), f(a)] instead. The map
◦
M(a, b) →Exit(a, b) obtained from any such homeomorphism is homotopic
to ιa,b.
The following proposition implies that Lurie’s exit path ∞-category construction (definition 2.3.21 and
theorem 2.3.29) applies to the stratification of X by the stable manifolds of (f, ξ). We will outline a proof
of it in section 3.2 (see corollary 3.2.9).
Proposition 2.4.33. The stratification of X by the stable manifolds of (f, ξ) is conical (in the sense of
definition 2.3.26).

2.4.3
The flow category of a Morse-Smale pair
Assume X is closed and let (f, ξ) be a Morse-Smale pair on X.
We defined previously the notion of
trajectory of ξ (definition 2.4.13). The goal of this section is to a introduce a notion of composition between
the trajectories of ξ.
Definition 2.4.34. A broken trajectory of ξ connecting a to b is a sequence γ0, . . . , γn of non-constant
unbroken trajectories of ξ, such that n ≥1 and there exist critical points a0 < a1 < . . . < an+1 such that
a0 = a, an+1 = b and for every 0 ≤i ≤n one has γi ∈
◦
M(ai, ai+1). We say that the broken trajectory is
broken at a1, a2, . . . , an−1 and an.
Example 2.4.35. See figure 11 for an example of broken trajectory.
Construction 2.4.36. Construction 2.4.29 extends to broken trajectories as follows: given a broken trajec-
tory γ0, . . . , γn connecting a to b as in definition 2.4.34 the parametrization of γ by the values of f is the
map
[f(b), f(a)] →X
sending s to γi(s) if s ∈[f(ai+1), f(ai)]. In other words, the embedding
◦
M(a, b)
−→
C0([f(b), f(a)], X),
γ
7−→
(s 7→γ(s))
extends to an injection from the set of all (broken and unbroken) trajectories connecting a to b, by concate-
nation on the right-hand side.
Definition 2.4.37. The set of broken and unbroken trajectories of ξ connecting a to b, endowed with the
topology induced by the compact-open topology on C0([f(b), f(a)], X), is called the space of trajectories of
ξ connecting a to b, and is denoted M(a, b).
Construction 2.4.38. Given three critical points a, b and c, the trajectories from a to b and from b to c
can be concatenated in a straighforward way, thus defining a continuous map
M(a, b) × M(b, c) →M(a, c).
This concatenation operation is (strictly) associative, by definition of broken trajectories. We further make
it unital, by declaring the units to be the constant trajectories at a critical point. We therefore make the
following definition.
Definition 2.4.39. The flow category of (X, f, ξ) is the topological category M whose set of objects is the
set of critical points of f, and whose space of morphisms between a and b is M(a, b). The composition
operation in M is defined as the concatenation of trajectories of ξ, as in construction 2.4.38.
This terminology was introduced by Cohen-Jones-Segal in [CJS95a], and is also used in the foundational
paper by Abouzaid-Blumberg [AB24].
The smooth structures on the spaces of unbroken trajectories connecting critical points (remark 2.4.17) can
be extended to the spaces of possibly broken trajectories as now recall. We first need a definition.
Definition 2.4.40. Let M be a smooth manifold with corners of dimension n. For every m ∈M there exists
a unique integer 0 ≤k ≤n such that a neighborhood of m in M is diffeomorphic to [0, +∞)k × Rn−k. The
point m is said to belong to the k-stratum of M.
65


## Page 66


The following result is proved in [Lat94, Proposition 2.11] and [Qin10, Theorem 3.3].
Proposition 2.4.41. For every pair of critical points (a, b) the space M(a, b) is compact and can be endowed
with a structure of smooth manifold with corners such that the following conditions are satisfied.
(i) The k-stratum is the set of trajectories that are a concatenation of k + 1 unbroken non-constant tra-
jectories (in other terms, that are broken exactly k times).
(ii) The smooth structure on M(a, b) extends that on
◦
M(a, b), in other words, the interior of M(a, b) is
the smooth manifold
◦
M(a, b).
(iii) These smooth structures are compatible in the sense that for every triple of critical points a, b, c, the
composition map
M(a, b) × M(b, c) →M(a, c)
is a smooth embedding.
As a consequence we obtain:
Corollary 2.4.42. Given two critical points a and b, the inclusion of the subspace of unbroken trajectories
between a and b, into M(a, b), is a homotopy equivalence.
Proof. By proposition 2.4.41, this is the inclusion of the interior of a smooth manifold with corners, hence
the result.
Remark 2.4.43. Proposition 2.4.41 also implies that the image of M(a, b) in C0([f(b), f(a)], X) is the
closure of that of

M(a, b).
2.5
Precise formulation of the main theorem
Using sections 2.1, 2.2, 2.3 and 2.4 we have all the ingredients to formulate precisely the main theorem of
this paper. To this end, we start by recalling the main objects involved.
Let X be a smooth closed manifold, (f, ξ) a Morse-Smale pair on X. Let M the associated flow category,
regarded as a topological category, and N(M) the homotopy coherent nerve of M.
Let A be the set of critical points of f endowed with the Smale ordering. Endow X with the A-stratification by
the stable manifolds of ξ and denote by SingA(X) the associated exit path ∞-category. Recall that SingA(X)
is endowed with the structure of an A-stratified simplicial set, i.e., a morphism SingA(X) →N(A).
The precise statement of our theorem will require two additional observations.
Firstly, N(M) is also naturally equipped with a morphism of simplicial sets N(M) →N(A) defined as
follows. Every n-simplex σ : ∆n →N(M) determines a sequence of n + 1 critical points a = (a0, . . . , an)
and, for every 0 ≤i ≤n −1, a morphism γi ∈M(ai, ai+1). In particular, M(ai, ai+1) is nonempty and
therefore the sequence a is increasing. The morphism N(M) →N(A) is defined by mapping σ to a.
Secondly, suppose given two critical points a, b of f. By proposition 2.2.40 we have a natural homotopy
equivalence between the morphism space of N(M) between a and b and the Kan complex Sing(M(a, b)). By
proposition 2.3.45 we have a natural isomorphism between the morphism space of SingA(X) between a and
b and the Kan complex Sing(Exit(a, b)). Recall from remark 2.4.31 the map ιa,b :
◦
M(a, b) →Exit(a, b). We
will prove in section 3 that this is a weak homotopy equivalence. Since the inclusion map
◦
M(a, b) →M(a, b)
66


## Page 67


is a homotopy equivalence (corollary 2.4.42), the induced morphism of Kan complexes Sing(
◦
M(a, b)) →
Sing(M(a, b)) is a homotopy equivalence. Altogether, we have an isomorphism in the homotopy category of
spaces between the Kan complexes of morphisms of N and SingA(X) between a and b.
Theorem (Precise formulation of theorem 1). There exists a canonical equivalence of ∞-categories N(M) →
SingA(X) that satisfies the following properties.
(i) This equivalence commutes with the functors to N(A) on both sides.
(ii) Suppose given two critical points a and b. At the level of the morphism spaces between a and b, this
equivalence induces the isomorphism constructed above.
Remark 2.5.1. Suppose given a functor G : N(M) →SingA(X) that satisfies property (i). The meaning
of property (ii) for such a functor is not obvious since we do not assume that G(a) = a and G(b) = b, so let
us clarify it. The functor G determines a map from the morphism space of N(M) between a and b to the
morphism space of SingA(X) between G(a) and G(b). Now, by condition (i), G(a) belongs to the a-stratum.
The space of morphisms of SingA(X) between a and G(a) is the space of paths in Xa starting at a and ending
at G(a). In particular, every such morphism is an isomorphism, and since Xa is contractible, this space of
morphisms is contractible. Therefore a and G(a) are canonically isomorphic as objects of SingA(X). The
same discussion holds with b instead of a. Consequently, the morphism spaces of SingA(X) between a and
b and between G(a) and G(b) are canonically homotopy equivalent. The functor G thus determines a map
between the morphism spaces of N(M) and SingA(X) between a and b that only depends on a contractible
space of extra choices.
The proof of this theorem is the purpose of sections 3 through 8, and will be given in section 8.2. We close
this section with two remarks.
Remark 2.5.2. We could consider alternatively the stratification of X by the unstable manifolds of ξ. These
are defined to be the stable manifolds of −ξ. Notice that −ξ is a negative pseudo-gradient vector field for −f
and that the Smale transversality condition for ξ is equivalent to the Smale transversality condition for −ξ.
Notice also that the flow category associated with (X, −f, −ξ) is canonically isomorphic to Mop. Theorem
1 therefore implies that there exists an equivalence between the ∞-category defined by Mop and the exit
path ∞-category associated with the stratification of X by the unstable manifolds of ξ, that satisfies the
corresponding properties.
Remark 2.5.3. In the statement of the theorem, by "canonical" we mean that the method that we follow
exhibits an equivalence of ∞-categories that is unique up to natural equivalence.

3
Spaces of exit paths as spaces of pseudo-gradient trajectories
Let X be a closed smooth manifold, (f, ξ) a Morse-Smale pair on X and A the poset of critical points of X.
Let a, b ∈A. Recall that in the previous section we constructed a continuous inclusion
ιa,b :
◦
M(a, b) →Exit(a, b)
of the space of unbroken trajectories of ξ from a to b, into the space of exit paths from a to b with respect
to the stratification X →A by the stable manifolds of ξ (construction 2.4.29 and lemma 2.4.30).
The main goal of this section is to prove the following proposition:
Proposition 3.0.1. The map ιa,b is a weak homotopy equivalence.
Recall that the space of morphisms of the exit path ∞-category SingA(X) between a and b has the homotopy
type of Exit(a, b) (proposition 2.3.45). Recall also that the space of morphism of M between a and b has
the homotopy type of
◦
M(a, b) (corollary 2.4.42). Together with proposition 3.0.1, this provides evidence for
our main theorem 1. This is also an essential ingredient of our proof of it.
In section 3.1, we review the definition of the Whitney conditions and their geometric interpretation. We
deduce from the latter the fact that the Whitney condition (b) for stratifications with two strata implies the
existence of a cylindrical structure, in the sense of section 2.3.4 (proposition 3.1.11). In section 3.2, we give
the general definition of a Whitney stratification and we state a result of Nicolaescu asserting that the Smale
transversality condition for the pseudo-gradient ξ is equivalent to the condition that the partition of X by
the stable manifolds of ξ is a Whitney stratification (theorem 3.2.3). We also outline a proof that Whitney
stratifications are conical (theorem 3.2.8). Finally, section 3.3 mostly consists of the proof of proposition
3.0.1. The argument combines properties of stratifications satisfying the Whitney condition (b) with the
description of the space of exit paths between the two strata of a cylindrically stratified space provided by
proposition 2.3.57.
3.1
Whitney conditions and cylindrical structure
In this section, we give a review of the theory of Whitney stratifications, and prove that Whitney stratifica-
tions with two strata are cylindrical. We follow [Nic07, Section 4] as well as [GWDPL06].
Although we will not use it, we begin by giving the classical definition of the Whitney conditions (a) and
(b).
Definition 3.1.1. Let n ≥0 and let Y, Y ′ be two disjoint submanifolds of Rn.

Remark 3.1.2. Condition (b) implies condition (a). Moreover, one can check that these two conditions are
independent of the choice of coordinates in the following sense. Given y ∈Y ∩Y ′ and a diffeomorphism φ
between an open neighborhood U of y in Rn, and an open subset V of Rn, the Whitney regularity condition
(a) (resp. (b)) is satisfied by the pair (Y ∩U, Y ′ ∩U) at y if and only if it is satisfied by its image by φ, at
the image of y. This allows to extend the definition of the Whitney regularity conditions to the case of two
disjoint submanifolds of any smooth manifold.
These two conditions have a geometrically insightful interpretation, which is the one that we will use in
practice. To explain it, we need to introduce the following notion of tubular neighborhood.
Definition 3.1.3. Let M be a smooth manifold and Y a smooth submanifold of M. A tubular neighborhood

3.2
Whitney stratifications
The general definition of a Whitney stratification is the following.
Definition 3.2.1. Let M be a smooth manifold and Z a locally closed subset of M. A Whitney stratification
of Z is a partition of Z into nonempty connected smooth submanifolds of M called the strata
Z =
G
s∈S
Zs
such that the following two conditions hold:
• The family {Zs} is locally finite, in the sense that every z ∈Z has a neighborhood in Z that intersects
only finitely many strata,
• for every s, t ∈S and every z ∈Zs ∩Zt, the pair (Zs, Zt) satisfies the Whitney regularity condition
(b) at z.
Notation 3.2.2. We will refer to a Whitney stratification of Z by indicating its set of strata S.
The notion of Whitney stratification is relevant for us because of the following result of Nicolaescu.
Theorem 3.2.3 (Nicolaescu). Let ξ be a negative pseudo-gradient adapted to a Morse function f : X →R,
where X is closed. The following two conditions are equivalent:
(i) ξ satisfies the Smale transversality condition,
(ii) the partition of X by the stable manifolds of ξ is a Whitney stratification of X (seen as a subset of
itself).
Proof. See [Nic07], theorems 4.32 and 4.33.
The purpose of the remaining of this section is to outline a proof that Whitney stratifications are conical
stratifications (theorem 3.2.8). This result is due to Thom in [Tho69]. To begin with, the following result
establishes a connection between the notion of Whitney stratification, and the notion of stratified topological
space from section 2.3.1.
Proposition 3.2.4. Let M be a smooth manifold and Z a locally closed subset of M endowed with a Whitney
stratification S. The relation ≤on S defined by
s ≤t ⇔Xs ∩Xt ̸= ∅
is a partial order. Moreover, the map Z →S that sends each point to the stratum to which it belongs is a
stratification of Z by (S, ≤), in the sense of definition 2.3.5.
The proof will be based on the following lemma.
Lemma 3.2.5. Suppose we have a Whitney stratification S of Z and two elements s, t ∈S such that
Zs ∩Zt ̸= ∅. Then Zs ⊂Zt.
Proof. See [GWDPL06], chapter II, corollary 5.7.
73


## Page 74


Proof of proposition 3.2.4. Let us first prove that ≤is a partial order. Reflexivity is immediate and tran-
sitivity follows from lemma 3.2.5. For antisymmetry, given s, t ∈S such that s ≤t and t ≤s, we wish to
prove that t = s. By lemma 3.2.5, we have Zt ⊂Zs and Zs ⊂Zt. Since the strata are disjoint, it suffices to
prove that Zs ∩Zt ̸= ∅. Let z ∈Zt. Since Zt is a smooth submanifold of M, there exists a neighborhood U
of z in M such that Zt ∩U is closed in U. Since Zt ⊂Zs, we have U ∩Zs ̸= ∅. Since Zs ⊂Zt and Zt ∩U is
closed in U, we have Zt ∩Zs ∩U ̸= ∅, in particular Zt ∩Zs is not empty.
Let us now prove that the partition
Z =
G
s∈S
Zs
is a stratification of Z by the poset (S, ≤). We need to prove that, for every s ∈S, Z≥s is open in Z. Let
t ≥s and z ∈Zt. There exists a neighborhood of z in Z intersecting a finite number of strata. Hence there
also exists a neighborhood U of z in Z having the property that, for every u ∈S such that Zt ∩Zu = ∅,
we have U ∩Zu = ∅. Hence for every u ∈S such that U ∩Zu ̸= ∅, we have u ≥t. Hence U ⊂Z≥t and
therefore also U ⊂Z≥s.
It is an essential feature of Whitney stratifications that the nice properties that hold in the two strata
case, which we reviewed in the previous section, extend in the presence of more than two strata. To make
this precise, we introduce the following definition. Notice that this definition is given in the topological
framework, although in this section we have worked in the smooth category so far. This is because we will
only use it to prove a conicality result, which is a topological notion.
Definition 3.2.6. Let Y be a connected topological space. A stratified fiber bundle over Y is a topological
fiber bundle p : E →Y with fiber F, such that E and F are both endowed with a stratification and the
following holds: there exists an open covering U of Y with local trivializations p−1(U) ≃U × F compatible
with the stratifications for every U ∈U (where p−1(U) is endowed with the stratification induced from that
of E, and U × F is endowed with the product stratification).
The following is a generalization of Ehresmann’s theorem to the Whitney stratified setting, known as Thom’s
first isotopy lemma. It first appeared in [Tho69].
Theorem 3.2.7. Let M be a smooth manifold and Z a locally closed subset of N endowed with a Whitney
stratification S. Suppose we have a connected smooth manifold Y and a smooth surjective map g : M →Y
such that for every s ∈S, g|Zs is a submersion and g|Zs∩Z is proper (where Zs denotes the closure of Zs in
M). Then g|Z is a stratified fiber bundle.
Proof. See [GWDPL06], chapter 2, theorem 5.2.
The following result is due to Mather ([Mat73, Theoerem 8.3]).
Theorem 3.2.8. Let N be a smooth manifold and Z a locally closed subset of M endowed with a Whitney
stratification S.
Endow S with the partial order from proposition 3.2.4 and regard Z as an S-stratified
topological space. Then (Z, S) is conically stratified.
Outline of proof. The proof follows the same lines as the proof given in section 3.1 that Whitney stratifica-
tions with two strata are cylindrical, except that one replaces proposition 3.1.7 with the more general version
provided by theorem 3.2.7.
More precisely, given z ∈Z, we wish to prove that (Z, S) is conically stratified at z. Let s ∈S be the
stratum of z. Since S is a Whitney stratification of Z, there exists an open neighborhood V of z in Zs, a

Corollary 3.2.9. Let ξ be a Morse-Smale negative pseudo-gradient vector field for a Morse function on a
smooth closed manifold X. The stratification of X by the stable manifolds of ξ is conical.
Proof. Combine theorem 3.2.3 with theorem 3.2.8.
Remark 3.2.10. The outline of proof of theorem 3.2.8 shows that a space l providing a local cone-like
description of a Whitney stratification near a point z ∈Zs can be obtained as follows. Consider a neigh-
borhood V of z in Zs and, given a tubular neighborhood T of V in the ambient manifold, with projection π
and radial function ρ, take l to be the intersection of Z with the sphere of radius ε centered at z, for ε > 0
sufficiently close to 0. Example 2.3.27 presents some pictures illustrating this remark.
Remark 3.2.11. Theorem 3.2.8 is an example of a property of a stratification involving possibly more than
two strata, that holds only assuming conditions on pairs of strata. In this sense, it is analogous to proposition
2.3.47.

As a consequence we have the following equivalent characterizations of the Smale ordering on the set of
critical points of f.
Corollary 3.3.6. Let a0, a1 be two critical points of f. The following conditions are equivalent:
(i) a0 ≤a1 in the sense of definition 2.4.21, i.e.,there exists an unbroken trajectory of ξ from a0 to a1,
(ii) there exists an exit path from a0 to a1,
(iii) the a0-stratum of X intersects the closure of the a1-stratum of X.
(iv) the a0-stratum of X is contained in the closure of the a1-stratum of X.
Proof. The equivalence (i) ⇔(ii) follows from proposition 3.0.1. The equivalence (ii) ⇔(iii) is true for any
conically stratified space, and therefore follows from corollary 3.2.9. The equivalence (iii) ⇔(iv) follows
from theorem 3.2.3 together with lemma 3.2.5.
80


## Page 81


4
The homotopy coherent nerve of a topological category
In this section, we define a functor F : sSet →CatTop called the geometric realization of the Leitch-Cordier
functor. Our motivation is that the homotopy coherent nerve functor N : CatTop →sSet, introduced in
section 2.2, is right adjoint to F. In particular for every topological category C we have by adjunction
N(C )n = HomsSet(∆n, N(C ))
= HomCatTop(F(∆n), C ).
In section 4.1 we define the functor F and discuss the adjunction between F and N.
The topological
categories F(∆n) are defined in a somehow unenlightening way, as the geometric realization of simplicial
categories whose morphism simplicial sets are nerves of posets. In section 4.2, we study F in more detail, and
give a more explicit description of the simplices of the homotopy coherent nerve of a topological category.
One consequence of our discussion is that an n-simplex of N(C) is a homotopy coherent composition of n
composable morphisms of C .
4.1
Definition
We begin by defining the Leitch-Cordier functor, following [Lur09, Section 1.1.5]. We start by defining it at
the level of the category ∆.
Recall from definition 2.3.31 that to every poset is associated a category, and the the nerve of the poset is the
nerve of this category. Moreover, the associated functor Pos →Cat as well as the nerve functor Cat →sSet
both commute with products. The nerve functor on posets Pos →sSet, which is the composition of the two
latter, therefore also commutes with products.
Definition 4.1.1. Let n ≥0 be an integer. We define a functor C : ∆→CatsSet as follows.
• The set of objects of C(∆n) is {0, . . . , n}.
• For 0 ≤i, j ≤n,
C(∆n)(i, j) =
ß ∅
if i > j,
nerve of Pn,i,j
if i ≤j,
where Pn,i,j denotes the set {E ⊂{0, . . . , n} : i, j ∈E and ∀k ∈E, i ≤k ≤j} partially ordered by the
inclusion.

Notation 4.1.2. For later use, we record the following notation. For every integer n ≥0, the simplicially
enriched category C(∆n) from definition 4.1.1 is defined by applying the nerve functor to the posets of
morphisms of a category enriched over posets. We denote the latter by P(∆n).
Definition 4.1.3. As the category of small simplicial categories admits all small colimits (appendix A), the
functor C admits a left Kan extension to sSet = Pre(∆) (construction 2.1.16). We still denote it C and refer
to it as the Leitch-Cordier functor.
The terminology comes from the fact that the functor C was constructed by Leitch in [Lei74] and further
studied by Cordier in [Cor82].
Notation 4.1.4. The right adjoint of the Leitch-Cordier functor (construction 2.1.19) is denoted N .
Let C be a simplicial category.
Since the geometric realization functor commutes with products when
considered with values in the category of compactly generated topological spaces, there is a topological
category |C| associated to C, defined by
ß Obj(|C|) = Obj(C),
|C|(X, Y ) = |C(X, Y )|.
This defines a functor | −| : CatsSet →CatTop. Moreover, since the singular simplicial set functor commutes
with products when taken with source the category of compactly generated topological spaces, to every
topological category C is associated a simplicial category Sing(C ) defined by
ß Obj(Sing(C )) = Obj(C ),
Sing(C )(X, Y ) = Sing(C (X, Y )).
The singular-realization adjunction between Top and sSet then holds between CatTop and CatsSet as well.
Definition 4.1.5. The composition
| −| ◦C : sSet →CatTop
is called the geometric realization of the Leitch-Cordier functor and is denoted by F.
It admits the following right adjoint:
Definition 4.1.6. The composition
N ◦Sing : CatTop →sSet
is called the homotopy coherent nerve functor and is denoted by N.

Definition 4.2.4. For 0 ≤i < k < j ≤n, a morphism γ ∈F(∆n)(i, j) is said to be broken at k if it can
be written as the composition of an element of F(∆n)(k, j) with an element of F(∆n)(i, k). It is said to be
unbroken if it cannot be written as a composition of two morphisms that are both different from the identity.
Corollary 4.2.5. Every morphism of F(∆n) can be written in a unique way as a composition of unbroken
morphisms.
Proof. By remark 4.2.2, it suffices to treat the case of a morphism γ ∈F(∆n)(0, n).
Using the home-
omorphism F(∆n)(0, n) ≃In−1 from (H) we write γ = (t1, . . . , tn−1).
By proposition 4.2.3, letting
0 < i1 < . . . < ik < n be the i’s such that ti = 1, ik+1 := 1, and γj ∈F(∆n)(ij, ij+1) the preimage
of (tij, . . . , tij+1) by the homeomorphism F(∆n)(ij, ij+1) ≃Iij+1−ij−1, we have that γj is unbroken and
γ = γk ◦. . . ◦γ0. Moreover, again by proposition 4.2.3, any decomposition of γ as a composition of unbroken
morphisms is uniquely determined by these properties.
Because of proposition 4.2.3, we can think of an n-simplex of N(C ) as a homotopy coherent composition of
n composable morphisms of C . We now explain this in detail for n = 0 to n = 3.
n = 0: F(∆0) is the topological category with one object and no morphism different from the identity, so a
0-simplex of N(C ) is the datum of an object of C .
n = 1: F(∆1) is the topological category with two objects and one morphism between them. Hence a
1-simplex of N(C ) is the datum of two objects of C and a morphism between them.
n = 2: A 2-simplex σ ∈N(C )2 is the datum of:
• Three objects X, Y and Z, the vertices of σ, respectively equal to the images of the three objects 0, 1
and 2 of F(∆2).
• Three morphisms, the faces of σ, as in the following diagram.

Corollary 4.2.7. Let φ : ∆n →∆m be a morphism in ∆and 0 ≤i ≤j ≤n. Let γ ∈F(∆n)(i, j) be an
unbroken morphism (in the sense of definition 4.2.4). Then F(φ)(i, j)(γ) ∈F(∆m)(φ(i), φ(j)) is unbroken.
Proof. Since face and degeneracy maps generate the morphisms of ∆, it suffices to treat the case when φ
is a face or degeneracy map, which we assume from now on. By remark 4.2.2, it suffices to treat the case
when i = 0 and j = n, which we also assume from now on. First, if φ(n) = φ(0) then the result follows
from the fact that F(∆m)(φ(0), φ(n)) = {id}. Otherwise, we have n > 0 and φ(n) > φ(0), and using the
homeomorphisms (H) we write γ = (t1, . . . , tn−1) and φ(0, n)(γ) = (sφ(0), . . . , sφ(n)−1). By propositions
4.2.1 and 4.2.6, if ti ̸= 1 for all 1 ≤i ≤n −1 then sj ̸= 1 for all φ(0) ≤i ≤φ(n) −1. The desired result
then follows from proposition 4.2.3.

The cubical stratified geometric realization
Let (f, ξ) be a Morse-Smale pair on a smooth closed manifold X, let A be the set of critical points of f
and M the associated flow category (see section 2.4 for the definitions of these objects). Given σ ∈N(M),
denote σ(i) = ai. Identifying F(∆n)(0, n) and In−1 using the homeomorphisms (H), we get a map σ(0, n) :
In−1 →M(a0, an). Moreover, any trajectory γ ∈M(a0, an) admits a natural parametrization by the interval
[f(an), f(a0)], denoted s 7→γ(s), and defined by the condition that f(γ(s)) = s (constructions 2.4.29 and
2.4.36). This defines an evaluation map
M(a0, an) × [f(an), f(a0)] −→X.

When trying to pass from the singular cube fσ in X to a singular stratified n-simplex in X, we encounter
two difficulties.
(i) As explained in section 1.4, fσ might visit more intermediary strata between a0 and an than only the
Xai’s. This is because nothing prevents the image of an unbroken morphism of F(∆n) by σ to be a
broken trajectory of ξ.
(ii) The domain of fσ cannot be identified with a simplex on the nose.
Recall that we endow A with the partial order given by the relation of being connected by a trajectory of ξ,
and we denote by ∆A the simplex category of the poset A (see 2.4.21 and 2.3.14 for precise definitions). In
this section, we address these two difficulties as follows:
• In section 5.1, we define an equivalence relation ∼a on In−1 × [f(an), f(a0)] for every ∆a ∈∆A. The
map fσ is compatible with this equivalence relation. Moreover, we define a functor

K →N(M), where K ∈sSet, is said to be unbroken if it factors through SM.
Remark 5.3.6. The two previous definitions admits the following reformulation: an unbroken simplex of
M is a functor σ : F(∆n) →M such that the associated map fσ : CA(∆a) →X (where the sequence
a = [a0 ≤. . . ≤an] is defined as ai = σ(i)) preserves stratifications.
The condition that a simplex of N(M) is unbroken can be alternatively formulated as follows:
Proposition 5.3.7. An n-simplex σ : F(∆n) →M is unbroken if and only if the image of every unbroken
morphism of F(∆n) (in the sense of definition 4.2.4) by σ is an unbroken trajectory.

Proof. Define an increasing sequence a by ai = σ(i).
The map fσ is stratified if and only if for every
(γ, s) ∈F(∆n)(0, n) × [f(an), f(a0)], writing γ = γk ◦. . . ◦γ0 as a composition of unbroken morphisms
with γj ∈F(∆n)(ij, ij+1), and letting l such that f(al+1) ≤s < f(al), we have that σ(γ)(s) is in the
stratum of ail+1. Since σ(γ)(s) = σ(γl)(s), the latter is equivalent to σ(γl) being unbroken, which proves the
statement.
Example 5.3.8. By proposition 5.3.7, a trajectory of ξ is unbroken if and only if the corresponding morphism
∆1 →N(M) is unbroken in the sense of definition 5.3.5.
We also have the following useful characterization of unbroken simplices.
Corollary 5.3.9. An n-simplex of N(M) is unbroken if and only if its restrictions to all faces of ∆n are
unbroken, and it maps the interior of F(∆n)(0, n) into the interior of M(a0, an).
Proof. Let γ be an unbroken morphism of F(∆n). By proposition 4.2.1, either γ is in the image of F(δ) for
some face δ with target ∆n, or γ is in the interior of F(∆n)(0, n), and is in particular unbroken. Moreover,
the interior of M(a0, an) consists precisely of the unbroken trajectories that connect a0 to an. We conclude
by applying proposition 5.3.7.

morphism spaces of F(∆n) and M.

Proof. If a is constant there is nothing to prove, so we suppose that is not, i.e. that a0 < an.

Firstly, π−1((sdA)≤b) = M(a0, an)\Z where Z is the set of trajectories that are broken at least once and
such that the critical points at which it is broken are not part of b. In other words Z is the union over the

M(a0, a1) × . . . × M(ak−1, an) →M(a0, an).

Z is closed in M(a0, an), hence π−1((sdA)≤b) is open, as desired.
Secondly, let us identify F(∆n)(0, n) with In−1. Then τ −1((sdA)≤b) = In−1\Z′ where Z′ is the set of those
(t1, . . . , tn−1) such that there exists 1 ≤j ≤n −1 such that aj is not part of b and tj = 1. This is a closed
subset of In−1, hence τ −1((sdA)≤b) is open, as desired.
Proposition 5.3.13. Let σ be an n-simplex of N(M). Define ∆a ∈∆A by ai = σ(i) and endow F(∆n)(0, n)
and M(a0, an) with the corresponding stratifications by (sdA)op. Then σ is unbroken if and only if σ(0, n)
is compatible with the stratifications.
Proof. σ(0, n) is compatible with the stratifications if and only if, for every γ ∈F(∆n)(0, n), σ(γ) is broken
at exactly as many points as γ. Writing γ as a composition of unbroken morphisms γ = γk ◦. . . ◦γ0, this is
equivalent to the condition that σ(γj) is unbroken for every 0 ≤j ≤k. We conclude by applying proposition
5.3.7.
Remark 5.3.14. One feature of the equivalent definition of the flow coherent nerve of M provided by
proposition 5.3.13, is that it does not rely on the stratified space X, but only on the topological category
M, together with extra structure on its morphism spaces. In section 9 we will exploit this property to define
the flow coherent nerve for a more general class of topological categories which we will introduce.

6
Comparison between the cubical and standard stratified geo-
metric realizations
It follows from the definition of SM (definition 5.3.3) that there are two morphisms of simplicial sets
N(M) ←SM →SingCA(X).
Recall that our ultimate goal is to compare the ∞-categories N(M) and SingA(X). With this in mind,
we state and prove in this section a comparison theorem between the usual stratified geometric realization
| −|A : ∆A →TopA, and its cubical counterpart CA (theorem 6.2.1). Recall that for every nonnegative
integer n we have
SingA(X)n = {σ : |∆a|A →X stratified for some a of length n}
and
SingCA(X)n = {σ : CA(∆a) →X stratified for some a of length n},
with the simplicial structures induced by the functorial structures of |−|A and CA respectively. In particular,
any comparison result between the functors | −|A and CA gives rise to a comparison result between the
simplicial sets SingA(X) and SingCA(X).
This section consists of three subsections. In section 6.1, we observe that a mere isomorphism of functors
does not exist, and we remedy this by introducing a modification of the functor | −|A, as well as restricting
ourselves to a subcategory of ∆A. In section 6.2, we state and prove the comparison result between | −|A
and CA (theorem 6.2.1). The proof relies on another result, theorem 6.2.8, whose proof is the subject of
section 6.3.
6.1
Modifying the standard stratified geometric realization
Ideally, we would like the functors | −|A and CA to be isomorphic. But observe that if a is a constant finite
sequence of elements of A, then CA(∆a) is a point, but on the other hand, if a is of length at least 1, then
|∆a|A is not a point and therefore CA(∆a) and |∆a|A are not homeomorphic. The goal of the present section
is to introduce a modification of the functor | −|A which better compares to CA.
Construction 6.1.1. Given a finite increasing sequence a of elements of A of length n, we say that a
constant subsequence of a of consecutive elements ap,q = [ap = ap+1 = . . . = aq] is of maximal length when,
if p > 0 then ap−1 ̸= ap and if q < n then aq ̸= aq+1. The inclusion of such a subsequence in a induces a
map |∆ap,q|A →|∆a|A in TopA. Let us declare any two elements in the image of this map to be equivalent.
This defines an equivalence relation on |∆a|A. We denote by |∆a|′
A the quotient of |∆a|A by this equivalence
relation.
Proposition 6.1.2. These equivalence relations on the values of |−|A are compatible with the stratifications
by A as well as with the maps induced by the morphisms of ∆A by functoriality. We have therefore constructed
a functor | −|′
A : ∆A →TopA.

6.2
The isomorphism of functors
In the previous section, we observed that the functors | −|A and CA where not isomorphic (as long as
dim X > 0). We introduced a quotient of the usual stratified geometric realization, which we denoted | −|′
A,
as well as a subcategory ∆+
A ⊂∆A. The goal of this section is to prove the following comparison theorem
between | −|A and CA.
Theorem 6.2.1. There exists an isomorphism of functors between the restrictions of | −|′
A and CA to ∆+
A.
Moreover, any two such isomorphisms are homotopic.
103


## Page 104


By a homotopy between two morphisms of functors from | −|′

| −|′

with the A-stratification induced by the first projection.
Remark 6.2.2. In [CJS95b, Section 3], Cohen, Jones and Segal considered two functors which, in our
notation, can be described as the functors
sd(∆+

obtained from | −|′

strictly increasing sequences, and corestricting along the forgetful functor TopA →Top (note that after this
restriction, | −|′
A coincides with | −|A). [CJS95b, Lemma 3.5] claims without proof that these two functors
are isomorphic. Theorem 6.2.1 establishes this claim.
Remark 6.2.3. We can actually prove that there exists an isomorphism which is compatible with the
respective filtrations on | −|′
A and CA induced by f (see remark 6.3.7 for more details). However, the proof
of our main result does not require this stronger statement, so we will only prove theorem 6.2.1 for the sake
of brevity.
Our strategy to prove theorem 6.2.1 is to proceed by induction on the length of finite sequences of consecutive
elements of A. More precisely, assume that we know that such an isomorphism exists in restriction to the
sequences of length at most n −1 (with n ≥1); we wish to extend it to sequences of length at most n.
We will see that for every ∆a ∈∆A, the image of ∂∆a by | −|′
A (resp. | −|CA) can be described as a
colimit over the faces of |∆a|′
A (resp. CA(∆a)) (corollary 6.2.7). Recall that the notion of face, to which
we refer here, was introduced in definition 6.1.13. In particular, if a is of length n, the induction hypothesis
provides a stratified homeomorphism |∂∆a|′
A ≃|∂∆a|CA. Towards the goal of performing the induction step
and proving that the latter extends to a stratified homeomorphism |∆a|′
A ≃CA(∆a), we will state theorem
6.2.8. It essentially reduces the question of finding such an extension to that of finding an extension of a
homeomorphism of Sn−1 to a homeomorphism of Dn, which is always possible.
This section is organised as follows. We begin by proving corollary 6.2.7. We then state theorem 6.2.8 and
from it we deduce the main theorem of this section, theorem 6.2.1. The proof of theorem 6.2.8 is deferred
to the next section.

by gluing together the faces of ∆n along their common boundary faces. More formally:

6.3
The proof of theorem 6.2.8
This section consists of the proof of theorem 6.2.8. It is divided into five paragraphs. The first one collects
some general facts about polytopes, a notion that will play an essential role in our proof. The second one
consists of a summary of the proof, while the remaining three complete the proof.
6.3.1
Polytopes
In this section, we record several definitions and results about polytopes. We use the book of Ziegler ([Zie12])
as a reference.
• A polytope is a nonempty topological subspace P of some Euclidean space Rd satisfying the following
two equivalent conditions.
(i) P is the convex hull of a finite set of points in Rd.
106


## Page 107


(ii) P is bounded and is the intersection of a finite family of closed affine halfspaces of Rd.
For a proof of the equivalence between these two conditions, see [Zie12, Theorem 1.1].
Example 6.3.1. For every nonnegative integer n, the topological space |∆n| ⊂Rn+1 is a polytope.
• Let P ⊂Rd be a polytope. The dimension of P is the dimension of the affine subspace of Rd spanned
by P.
• Let P ⊂Rd be a polytope. Given c ∈Rd, we say that the inequality ⟨c, x⟩≤c0 is valid for P if it is
satisfied for every point x ∈P (where ⟨·, ·⟩denotes the Euclidean scalar product). A face of P is a
subset of P of the form {x ∈P | ⟨c, x⟩= c0} for some valid inequality ⟨c, x⟩≤c0.
• Taking c = c0 = 0, we see that P is a face of itself. A face of P that is different from P is called a
proper face.
• The boundary of P is the union of the proper faces of P. It is denoted ∂P. The interior of P is P\∂P.
It is denoted Int(P). If P ⊂Rd and P has dimension d, these respectively coincide with the boundary
of P in Rd, and the interior of P in Rd.
• The intersection of two faces of a polytope is a face ([Zie12, Proposition 2.3]).
• A face of a polytope is a polytope ([Zie12, Proposition 2.3]). The dimension of a face is the dimension
of the corresponding polytope.
Remark 6.3.2. We warn the reader of the following conflict of terminology: for every n ≥1, we
have already introduced a notion of face of ∆n ∈sSet (notation and terminology 2.1.8), as well as a
corresponding notion of face of |∆n| ∈Top (definition 6.1.14). The latter coincides with the notion of
(n −1)-dimension face of |∆n| regarded as an n-dimensional polytope.
• A vertex of a polytope is a face of dimension 0, that is, a face that is reduced to a point. Every polytope
is the convex hull of its vertices ([Zie12, Proposition 2.2]).
The following lemma about polytopes will be essential to us.
Lemma 6.3.3. Let P ⊂Rd be a d-dimensional polytope and p a point belonging to the interior of P. Let
q ∈Rd be a point different from p. The intersection of ∂P with the half-line p + R+(q −p) is a point.
Proof. We denote this half-line by l. It can be identified with R+ and in particular it inherits an order
relation from R+.
We start by proving that l ∩∂P ̸= ∅. Since P is bounded, the set {r ∈l | r /∈P} is nonempty, and we define
m to be its infimum. Since the interior of P is open in Rd, we have m /∈Int(P), and since P is closed in Rd
we have m ∈P. Hence m ∈∂P.
We conclude by proving that l ∩∂P is reduced to a point. Let m be the infimum of l ∩∂P; since l ∩∂P is
closed we have m ∈l ∩∂P. Let us write P as a finite intersection of affine closed halfspaces of Rd
P =
\
1≤i≤n
{x ∈Rd | ⟨ci, x⟩≤c0,i}.
Since m ∈∂P, there exists 1 ≤j ≤n such that ⟨cj, m⟩= c0,j. Since p lies in the interior of P we have
⟨cj, p⟩< c0,j, and since p ∈l and p < m we deduce that ⟨cj, m′⟩> c0,j for every m′ ∈l such that m′ > m.
In particular, for every m′ ∈l such that m′ > m, we have m′ /∈P, as desired.

6.3.2
Summary of the proof
Let us fix a nonconstant sequence a = [a0 ≤. . . ≤an] of elements of A, of length n. In this section, we
summarize the proof of theorem 6.2.8. We start with the case G = | −|′
A. For each maximally constant
subsequence [ap = ap+1 = . . . = aq] ⊂a, i.e.,satisfying ap−1 < ap if p ≥1 and aq+1 > aq is q ≤n −1, the
convex hull of the set of vertices {ep, ep+1, . . . , eq} of the polytope |∆n| is a face of |∆n|. By virtue of the
assumption that a is not constant, this face is proper. By construction of the functor | −|′
A (construction
6.1.1), the space |∆a|′
A is obtained from |∆n| by collapsing each of these proper faces to a point. Note that
these faces are disjoint. We will prove the following lemma.
Lemma 6.3.5. Let P be a polytope, F ⊂P a proper face and U a neighborhood of F in P. There exists a
map c : P →P satisfying the following properties.
(i) The map c is the identity outside of U.
(ii) The map c is constant on F.
(iii) The resulting map P/F →P is a homeomorphism.
(iv) This homeomorphism restricts to a homeomorphism ∂P/F ≃∂P.
This lemma will be a consequence of an improved statement (lemma 6.3.10), whose proof will be given in
section 6.3.3. The proof of theorem 6.2.8 in the case G = CA will follow from this lemma together with
the fact that an n-dimensional polytope is homeomorphic to an n-dimensional disc (proposition 6.3.4); the
details can be found in section 6.3.3.
We now summarize the proof of theorem 6.2.8 in the case when G = CA. As in the case when G = |−|′
A, the
space G(∆a) is obtained from a polytope (namely the polytope In−1 × [f(an), f(a0)]) by modding out by
an equivalence relation. However, in general, passing to the quotient does not amount to collapsing disjoint
faces, nor to collapsing full faces, which leads to extra difficulties compared to the case G = | −|′
A. In order
to establish the desired result, we will make use of the filtration on CA(∆a) induced by f (construction

This map can be precisely defined as follows. Declare (1, 1) to be mapped to (0, 1), and the other
vertices of I2 to be mapped to themselves. Then, write every point (t1, t2) ∈I2 as the following convex
combination of the vertices of I2
(t1, t2) = (1 −t1)(1 −t2) × (0, 0) + t1(1 −t2) × (1, 0) + (1 −t1)t2 × (0, 1) + t1t2 × (1, 1)
and extend to I2 by linearity on such convex combinations. This way of decomposing an element
(t1, t2) ∈I2 as a convex combination of the vertices of I2 extends to higher dimensional cubes, yielding
what we call the C-representation, which is the subject of section 6.3.4. Note that already in dimension
2, there is not a unique way of decomposing an element (t1, t2) ∈I2 as a convex combination of the
vertices of I2 in general (see remark 6.3.20).

## Page 113


Point (ii) of proposition 6.3.8, combined with the fact that a (n −1)-dimensional polytope is homeomorphic
to a (n−1)-dimensional disc, implies that the map fCA(∆a) is a (n−1)-dimensional disc bundle in restriction
to the complement of the set of critical values {f(an), f(an−1), . . . , f(a0)} ⊂[f(an), f(a0)]. In view of item
(iii) of proposition 6.3.8, in order to complete the proof, we need to answer the following question: given a
polytope P and a proper face F ⊂P, how can we describe the quotient space P × [0, 1]/(F × {1})? This
will be done thanks to the following improvement of lemma 6.3.5.
Lemma 6.3.10. Let P be a polytope, F ⊂P a proper face and U a neighborhood of F in P. There exists
a map h : P × [0, 1] →P × [0, 1] satisfying the following properties.
• The map h commutes with the projection to [0, 1]. In other words, there is a [0, 1]-family of maps
ht : P →P such that h(x, t) = (ht(x), t).
• The map h is the identity outside of U × [0, 1].
• The map h1 : P →P is constant on F and the resulting map P × [0, 1]/(F × {1}) →P × [0, 1] is a
homeomorphism.
• This homeomorphism restricts to a homeomorphism ∂P × [0, 1]/(F × {1}) ≃∂P × [0, 1].
The proof of this lemma is the purpose of section 6.3.3.
Remark 6.3.11. Lemma 6.3.10 implies lemma 6.3.5: indeed, given a map h as in lemma 6.3.10, the map
c = h1 fulfills the conditions of lemma 6.3.5.
We deduce that the images by f of the element of the sequence a form a finite sequence of real numbers
f(an) = λk < λk−1 < . . . < λ1 < λ0 = f(a0) such that fCA(∆a) is a (n −1)-dimensional disc bundle over
(λk, λk−1], over [λ1, λ0) as well as over [λj+1, λj] for every 1 ≤j ≤k −2. Now, we have the following lemma.
Lemma 6.3.12. Let k ≥2 be an integer, consider two real numbers λ0, λk such that λk < λ0, and suppose
we are given a topological space E and a map p : E →(λk, λ0). Suppose further that there exists a sequence
of real numbers λk < λk−1 < . . . < λ1 < λ0 such that p is a (n −1)-dimensional disc bundle over (λk, λk−1],
over [λ1, λ0) as well as over [λj+1, λj] for every 1 ≤j ≤k −2. Then, p is a (n −1)-dimensional disc bundle
over (λk, λ0).
In section 6.3.6, we prove this lemma and collect all the ingredients to conclude the proof of theorem 6.3.6.
6.3.3
A collapsing lemma for polytopes
The goal of this section is to prove lemma 6.3.10, and use this lemma to complete the proof of theorem 6.2.8
in the case G = | −|′
A.
To begin with, the proof of lemma 6.3.10 will be done in three steps.
Step 1: Denoting by k the dimension of P, we will prove that every neighborhood of F in ∂P contains a
neighborhood of F in ∂P that is homeomorphic to Rk−1 through a homeomorphism that identifies F with
a polytope in Rk−1.
Step 2: We will prove an analogue of lemma 6.3.10 with (P, F) replaced by (Rk−1, Q), where Q ⊂Rk−1 is a
polytope. Combining this and step 1, we will deduce an analogue of lemma 6.3.10 with (P, F) replaced by
(∂P, F).
Step 3: We will deduce the desired result from step 2.
Step 1: The goal of this step is to prove the following lemma

## Page 114


Lemma 6.3.13. Let P ⊂Rd be a k-dimensional polytope, F ⊂P a proper face and U a neighborhood of F
in ∂P. There exists a neighborhood V of P in U and a homeomorphism V ≃Rk−1 that identifies F with a
polytope in Rk−1.
Proof. Up to restricting ourselves to the affine hull of P in Rd, we can assume that k = d, which we do from
now on. Let ⟨c, x⟩≤M be a valid inequality for P such that F = {p ∈P | ⟨c, p⟩= M} (see section 6.3.1 for
the definition of a valid inequality). We denote by λc the linear form on Rd defined by x 7→⟨c, x⟩. Given a
subset E ⊆Rd and a real number s, we denote by Es the set of those e ∈E such that λc(e) = s, so that in
particular, we have F = PM. Similarly, given a real number s′, we also use the notation E>s, E≤s′
>s .
Since F is a proper face of P, we have P ̸= PM. Consequently, the subset λc(P) ⊂R is a closed bounded
interval with nonempty interior, whose maximum is M. Moreover the set λc(cU) is a closed subset of λc(P)
which does not contain M. In particular, there exists a point s ∈λc(P) such that s /∈λc(cU) and s < M.
Denote V = (∂P)>s; this is an open neighborhood of F in ∂P which is contained in U.
We proceed by constructing a homeomorphism between V and Rd−1 that satisfies the desired condition. To
this end, we start by justifying that there exists a point p ∈(Int(P))s. For this, denote λc(P) by [m, M].
Since s ∈(m, M), it suffices to show that λc(Int(P)) = (m, M). Now, we observe two things, which follow
from the description of P as a finite intersection of affine closed halfspaces of Rd.
• The interior of P is connected. Since λc is an open map, we deduce that λc(Int(P)) is an open interval.
• P is the closure of Int(P). Therefore, λc(P) is the closure of λc(Int(P)). This forces λc(Int(P)) =
(m, M).
Fix p ∈(Int(P))s from now on and consider the subspace (Rd)≤M
>s ⊂Rd. There is a map (Rd)≤M
>s →(Rd)M
that carries a point x to the intersection of the half-line p + R+(x −p) with (Rd)M. We denote by φ its
restriction to V ; we regard φ as a map V →Rd−1 by using an affine identification between (Rd)M and Rd−1.
We will prove that φ is a homeomorphism satisfying the desired property.
First note that since F ⊂(Rd)M, the map φ is the identity on F, and therefore the image of F by φ is a
polytope in Rd−1. Second, φ is bijective; indeed, for every point x ∈(Rd)M, the half-line p + R+(x −p)
intersects ∂P at a unique point by lemma 6.3.3, and this point has to belong to V .
To conclude that φ is a homeomorphism, we observe that φ extends to a bijective map between the one-point
compactifications of V and Rd−1. Since these one-point compactifications are Hausdorff, this extension is a
homeomorphism, and therefore φ as well.
Step 2: The goal of this step is to prove two analogues of lemma 6.3.10.
Lemma 6.3.14. Let d ≥1 be an integer, Q ⊂Rd a polytope and U a neighborhood of Q in Rd. There exists
a map h : Rd × [0, 1] →Rd × [0, 1] satisfying the following properties.
• The map h commutes with the projection to [0, 1]. In other words, there is a [0, 1]-family of maps
ht : Rd →Rd such that h(x, t) = (ht(x), t).
• The map h is the identity outside of U × [0, 1].
• The map h1 : Rd →Rd is constant on Q and the resulting map Rd × [0, 1]/(Q × {1}) →Rd × [0, 1] is
a homeomorphism.

Corollary 6.3.16. Let P be a d-dimensional polytope and F ⊂P a proper face. The pair (P/F, ∂P/F) is

Remark 6.3.20. The polytope IN is the convex hull of the set {0, 1}N, that is, every map ∆2N−1 →IN
that is bijective on vertices and linear, is surjective. The C-representation can be regarded as a section of
such a map. However, as long as N ≥2, such a section is not unique. For instance, a different section can
be obtained by choosing a triangulation of IN whose set of vertices is {0, 1}N, and writing every element of
IN as a convex combination of the vertices of a simplex of the triangulation in which it lies.
Notation 6.3.21. For every t ∈RN, we denote by VN(t) the subset of VN formed by those vertices for
which the corresponding coefficient in the C-representation of t is nonzero. In other words, for every v ∈VN,
we have v ∈VN(t) except if vj = 0 and tj = 1 or if vj = 1 and tj = 0.

a.

Proof. This is a consequence of Geršgorin’s theorem ([Var11, Theorem 1.1]) which asserts that for any
complex square matrix A = (ai,j) of size N and every eigenvalue λ ∈C of A, there exists an integer
1 ≤k ≤N such that the following inequality holds

If, in the assumption on M, one asks for the large inequality only, this implies that the real part of every
eigenvalue of M is nonnegative. If one asks for the strict inequality for every 1 ≤k ≤N, this implies that
the real part of every eigenvalue of M is strictly positive. Since the determinant of M is the product of its
eigenvalues, and the non-real ones occur in conjugate pairs, the result follows.

7
Unbroken simplices of N(M)
In this section, we consider the flow category M of a Morse-Smale pair (f, ξ) on a smooth closed manifold X.
We study the flow coherent nerve of M (definition 5.3.3) denoted SM. We begin by proving in section 7.1
that this is an ∞-category (proposition 7.1.1). Then, in section 7.2, we prove that the inclusion SM →N(M)
is an equivalence of ∞-categories (proposition 7.2.1).
In the next two sections, we carry out the comparison between SM and SingA(X) initiated in section 6.
In section 7.3, we prove that the simplicial set SingCA(X) is an ∞-category (proposition 7.3.4). In section
7.4, we introduce an ∞-subcategory SingCA,r(X) ⊆SingCA(X) (definition 8.1.1) such that the morphism
SM →SingCA(X) corestricts to a morphism SM →SingCA,r(X), and we prove that the latter is an
equivalence of ∞-categories (proposition 7.4.5).
7.1
Filling inner horns in the flow coherent nerve of M
The goal of this section is to prove the following proposition.
Proposition 7.1.1. The flow coherent nerve SM of M is an ∞-category.
The proof of proposition 7.1.1 will require some preliminaries. We use the characterization of ∞-categories
in terms of the inner horn filling property (definition 2.2.15). We thus wish to prove that for every n ≥0
and 0 < k < n, every lifting problem of the form
Λn
k

7.2
The equivalence between the homotopy-and flow coherent nerves of M
The goal of this section is to prove the following proposition.
Proposition 7.2.1. The functor induced by the inclusion iM : SM →N(M) is an equivalence of ∞-
categories.
Our strategy is to apply the characterization of equivalences of ∞-categories in terms of essential surjectivity
and full faithfulness (theorem 2.2.29), and reduce the full faithfulness condition to the result that for every
pair of critical points, the inclusion of the space of unbroken trajectories connecting these, into the space of
broken ones, is a homotopy equivalence (corollary 2.4.42).
We will use a model for the morphism spaces of an ∞-category which is different from that of definition
2.2.26. We start by introducing it, following [Lur09, Section 1.2.2].
Construction 7.2.2. For every arrow φ : ∆n →∆m in ∆, there is a corresponding arrow ∆n+1 →∆m+1
defined to be φ in restriction to {0, . . . , n}, and carrying n+1 to m+1. Consider the simplicial sets ∆n+1/∆n
and ∆m+1/∆m obtained by collapsing the faces opposite to the vertices n + 1 and m + 1 respectively (an
explicit description of these simplicial sets follow from the general description of colimits of simplicial sets
given in section 2.1). This arrow passes to the quotient and determines an arrow ∆n+1/∆n →∆m+1/∆m.
This turns the assignment ∆n 7→∆n+1/∆n into a functor ∆→sSet.
We denote this functor by K.
Furthermore, for every ∆n ∈∆, the simplicial set K(∆n) has two vertices: one corresponding to the
collapsed face of ∆n+1, and one corresponding to the vertex n + 1 of ∆n+1. We denote these by xn and yn
respectively. Finally, we denote by SingK(−) the right adjoint of the left Kan extension of K to sSet. By
definition, for every simplicial set T we have
SingK(T)n = Hom(K(∆n), T).
Definition 7.2.3. Let T ∈sSet be an ∞-category and x, y two objects of T. The right morphism space of
T between x and y, denoted HomR
T (x, y), is the simplicial subset of SingK(T) formed by those morphisms
K(∆n) →T sending xn to x and yn to y. In particular, for every nonnegative integer n, HomR
T (x, y)n is the
set of those morphisms ∆n+1 →T that are constant equal to x on the face opposite to the vertex n + 1, and
that carry the vertex n + 1 to y.
140


## Page 141


Proposition 7.2.4. For every x, y ∈T, HomR
T (x, y) is a Kan complex.
Proof. See [Lur09, Proposition 1.2.2.3].
Our next step is to state a comparison result between the notion of right morphism space, and the notion
of morphism space from definition 2.2.26. Recall that the n-simplices of HomT (x, y) correspond to certain
maps ∆n × ∆1 →T, while the n-simplices of HomR
T (x, y) correspond to certain maps ∆n+1 →T. The
simplicial set ∆n × ∆1 is naturally identified with the nerve of the poset [n] × [1], while the simplicial set
∆n+1 is naturally identified with the nerve of the poset [n + 1]. There is a morphism of simplicial sets
∆n × ∆1 →∆n+1 defined to be the nerve of the morphism of posets [n] × [1] →[n + 1] defined as (i, 0) 7→i
and (i, 1) 7→n + 1 for every 0 ≤i ≤n. These morphisms are natural in n and thus give rise to a morphism
of simplicial sets HomR
T (x, y) →HomT (x, y).
Proposition 7.2.5. This morphism of simplicial sets is natural with respect to T. Moreover, if T is an
∞-category, this is a homotopy equivalence.
Proof. Naturality follows from the construction. For the rest, see [Lur09, Corollary 4.2.1.8].
Corollary 7.2.6. Let F : C →D be a functor between ∞-categories. The functor F is essentially surjective
if and only if for every pair of objects x, y ∈C, the induced morphism of Kan complexes
HomR
C (x, y) →HomR
D(F(x), F(y))
is a homotopy equivalence.
Our next goal is to better understand the right morphism spaces of the ∞-category N(M). We closely
follow [Lur09, Section 2.2.2].
The description of these right morphism spaces will involve the following construction.
Construction 7.2.7. For every integer n ≥0, we denote by Q(∆n) the topological space F(K(∆n))(xn, yn)
(recall that F is the Leitch-Cordier functor and that K(∆n), xn and yn where introduced in construction
7.2.2). Note that for every arrow φ : ∆n →∆m in ∆one has F(K(∆n))(φ)(xn) = xm and F(K(∆n))(φ)(yn) =
ym. We therefore have a map F(K(∆n))(φ) : Q(∆n) →Q(∆m). This turns the assignment ∆n 7→Q(∆n)
into a functor Q : ∆7→Top. We denote by SingQ the right adjoint of the left Kan extension of this functor
to sSet. Recall that for every topological space Y , the set of n-simplices of SingQ(Y ) is Hom(Q(∆n), Y ).
Fix a nonnegative integer n and two critical points a, b ∈M. By definition, an n-simplex of HomR
N (M)(a, b)
is a morphism of simplicial sets
K(∆n) →N(M)
sending xn to a and yn to b. By adjunction, this is the same as a functor of topological categories

the topological category F(K(∆n)).
Proposition 7.2.8. The topological category F(K(∆n)) is described as follows.
• It has two objects xn and yn.

Proof. Since the functor F commutes with colimits, F(K(∆n)) is canonically isomorphic to the quotient

Denote by C the topological category described in the statement of the proposition. There is a functor

Definition 7.4.2. We define the restricted version of SingCA(X) to be the simplicial subset of SingCA(X)
formed by those simplices whose vertices are critical points of f. We denote it by SingCA,r(X).
The restricted version of SingCA(X) satisfies the following two properties.
Proposition 7.4.3. The simplicial set SingCA,r(X) is an ∞-category.

SingCA(X), this follows from the fact that SingCA(X) is an ∞-category (proposition 7.3.4).

The main result of this section is the following.
Proposition 7.4.5. The functor s(X,A)
M
: SM →SingCA,r(X) is an equivalence of ∞-categories.
As for the proof of proposition 7.2.1, our strategy is to prove that this functor is essentially surjective and

morphism spaces of these two ∞-categories. This time, our plan is to reduce the full faithfulness condition to
the condition that for every pair of critical points (a, b), the inclusion, denoted ιa,b, of the space of unbroken
trajectories between a and b into the space of exit paths between a and b (construction 2.4.29), is a weak
homotopy equivalence (proposition 3.0.1).
Fix two critical points a, b ∈M such that a < b. Recall from definition 7.2.3 that the right morphism space
of SingCA,r(X) between a and b is a simplicial subset of SingK(SingCA,r(X)). In this description, we forget
the stratification on SingCA,r(X) and regard it as a mere simplicial set.

of the simplicial sets ∆n+1 and ∆m+1 respectively. These stratifications pass to the quotients K(∆n) and
K(∆m), and are compatible with the map K(φ). We denote the resulting functor ∆→sSetA by Ka,b. We
denote by SingKa,b the right adjoint of the left Kan extension of this functor to sSet. Recall that for every
A-stratified simplicial set Y , SingKa,b(Y ) is the simplicial set whose set of n-simplices is Hom(Ka,b(∆n), Y ).
The right morphism space of SingCA,r(X) between a and b can be described as follows.
Proposition 7.4.7. The simplicial set HomR
SingCA,r(X)(a, b) is identified with the following simplicial subset
SingKa,b(SingCA,r(X)) ⊂SingK(SingCA,r(X)).
Proof. By definition, an n-simplex σ : K(∆n) →SingCA,r(X) of the right-hand side of this inclusion, belongs
to this right morphism space if and only if it sends xn to a and yn to b. At the level of 0-simplices, the a and

therefore equivalent to the condition that σ sends xn to the a-stratum, and yn to the b-stratum. By remark
2.3.37, this is equivalent to the condition that σ is compatible with the A-stratification on Ka,b(∆n), as
desired.

Suppose given two ∞-categories C and D and a morphism of semi-simplicial sets G : C →D. We can think

necessarily unital, in the sense that G does not necessarily carry identity morphisms to identity morphisms.
