9
Stratified categories as exit path categories
Let M be a topological category (i.e., for us, a topologically enriched category) and Y a stratified topo-
logical space whose stratified singular simplicial set is an ∞-category (when Y is metrizable, an equivalent
formulation of the latter condition is given by proposition 2.3.47).
The goal of this section is to explain how the techniques developed in this paper can be reproduced to prove
that, under certain further assumptions, the ∞-category defined by M is equivalent to the ∞-category
defined by the stratified singular simplicial set of Y . The main theorem of this section (theorem 9.3.8) does
not refer to Morse theory, but instead, to an extra structure on M inspired from Morse flow categories,
which we refer to as a structure of stratified category. In the particular case of Morse flow categories, this
theorem recovers our main theorem 1. This section is intended to be readable as a way to understand the
proof of theorem 1. The proofs of most of the results of this section are straightforward adaptations of proofs
given in the previous sections, in which case these are simply referred to, and not reproduced.
We will study other applications of the main theorem of this section in future work.
9.1
Stratified categories
Our goal in this section is to introduce an extra structure on M that allows to speak of unbroken morphisms
of M and, more generally, to define the flow coherent nerve of M. To this end, we will follow the equiv-
alent definition of the flow coherent nerve of a Morse flow category provided by proposition 5.3.13, which
makes use of stratifications on the spaces of morphisms. We will introduce the notion of stratified category
to be a topological category whose topological spaces of morphisms are coherently endowed with certain
stratifications. In the next section, we will define and study the flow coherent nerve of a stratified category.
In order to formulate the precise definition of a stratified category, we start by explaining a construction
that associates, to every poset, a category enriched over posets. This construction can also been found in
[Lur, Construction 2.3.5.1].
Construction 9.1.1. Let A be a poset. We construct from A a poset-enriched category PA, called the path
category of A, as follows. The set of objects of PA is A. Given a, b ∈A, the poset of morphisms of PA
between a and b is the full subposet of (sdA)op spanned by those sequences starting with a and finishing
with b. In other words, this is the set of strictly increasing sequences of elements of A of the form
a = a0 < a1 < . . . < an−1 < an = b
endowed with the partial order defined by a ≤a′ if and only if a′ is a subsequence of a. To define the
composition operation of PA, consider two sequences
a = [a = a0 < . . . < an = b] ∈PA(a, b)
and
b = [b = b0 < . . . < bm = c] ∈PA(b, c).
The composition of a and b is defined as the concatenation of a and b, i.e.,as the sequence
b ◦a = [a0 < . . . < an < b1 < . . . < bm].
The construction of the path category of a poset allows us to make the following definition. This definition
was partly inspired by the definition of abstract flow categories by Abouzaid-Blumberg in [AB24, Sections 2
and 3].
Definition 9.1.2. Let M be a topological category and A the set of objects of M. A structure of stratified
category on M consists of the datum of a partial order on A together with a functor of topological categories
155


## Page 156


Strat : M →PA, where PA is regarded as a topological category by endowing each of the posets PA(a, b)
with the Alexandrov topology (definition 2.3.3). Moreover, Strat is assumed to induce the identity on A.
The triple consisting of the topological category M, the poset A and the functor Strat is called a stratified
category and denoted (M, A, Strat).
Remark 9.1.3. Suppose we are given a stratified category (M, A, Strat) and two objects a, b of M. We
have PA(a, b) ̸= ∅if and only if a ≤b. In particular, since there is a map Strat(a, b) : M(a, b) →PA(a, b),
if M(a, b) ̸= ∅then we must have a ≤b.
Notation 9.1.4. Suppose we are given a stratified category (M, A, Strat) and two objects a, b ∈A. If the
poset PA(a, b) is nonempty, i.e.,if a ≤b, then it has a maximal element. If a = b, this is the sequence [a],
and if a < b, this is the sequence [a < b]. We denote by
◦
M(a, b) the stratum of M(a, b) associated with this
maximal element, with respect to the stratification defined by the map Strat(a, b) : M(a, b) →PA(a, b).
Definition 9.1.5. Suppose we are given a stratified category (M, A, Strat) and a morphism γ of M from
an object a to an object b. The morphism γ is said to be unbroken if γ ∈
◦
M(a, b).
We have encountered two sources of examples of stratified categories in this paper.
Example 9.1.6. Let A be a poset. Applying the nerve functor to the morphism posets of PA, one associates
to PA a simplicially enriched category. Applying the geometric realization functor to the simplicial sets of
morphisms of the latter, one associates to PA a topological category, which we denote by |PA|. We can endow
|PA| with the structure of a stratified category as follows. For every a, b ∈A, the nerve of the poset PA(a, b)
is endowed with a tautological PA(a, b)-stratification (defined by the identity morphism N(PA(a, b)) →
NA(PA(a, b))), which in turn endows the geometric realization |N(PA(a, b))| with the structure of a PA(a, b)-
stratified topological space.
We let StratA(a, b) denote the stratification map |N(PA(a, b))| →PA(a, b).
These stratifications are compatible with compositions. We have thus a stratified category (|PA|, A, StratA).
Remark 9.1.7. In section 4 we studied the left adjoint of the homotopy coherent nerve functor, which we
denoted F : sSet →CatTop. For every nonnegative integer n, the topological category F(∆n) is defined
by applying the nerve functor, and then the geometric realization functor, to the posets of morphisms of a
poset-enriched category P(∆n) (notation 4.1.2). On the other hand, consider the stratified category obtained
from example 9.1.6 in the special case when A is the ordered set [n] = (0 < 1 < . . . < n). The poset-enriched
categories P[n] and P(∆n) are almost the same except that we made the choice to define the partial orders
on the morphism sets of P(∆n) to be the opposite of those on the morphism sets of P[n]. Example 9.1.6
endows P[n] with a structure of stratified category; we can also endow F(∆n) with the structure of a stratified
category as follows. For every 0 ≤i, j ≤n, P(i, j) is the set {E ⊂{0, . . . , n} : i, j ∈E and ∀k ∈E, i ≤k ≤j}
partially ordered by the inclusion relation, while P[n](i, j) is (naturally isomorphic to) the same set partially
ordered by the reverse inclusion relation. There is a natural isomorphism between these two posets defined in
both directions by mapping E to {i, j}∪{i < k < j | k /∈E}. This defines an isomorphism of poset-enriched
categories P(∆n) ≃P[n]. The structure of stratified category (|P[n]|, [n], Strat[n]) thus induces a structure
of stratified category on F(∆n), which we denote by (F(∆n), [n], Stratn). One can check that the notion of
unbroken morphism of F(∆n) from definition 9.1.5 coincides with that of definition 4.2.4, i.e., a morphism
of F(∆n) is unbroken if and only if it cannot be written as a composition of two morphisms that are both
different from the identity.
Example 9.1.8. Let (f, ξ) be a Morse-Smale pair on a smooth closed manifold. Denote by M the associated
flow category and by A the associated poset of critical points. In section 5.3 we defined for every a, b ∈A a
stratification map M(a, b) →PA(a, b), which associates to a trajectory between a and b the ordered sequence
of critical points at which it is broken, including a and b (see proposition 5.3.12). Denoting this stratification
map by StratM(a, b), we have a stratified category (M, A, StratM). The notion of unbroken morphism of
M from definition 9.1.5 coincides with the notion of unbroken trajectory of ξ connecting two critical points.
We now introduce the notion of functor between stratified categories. We start by a construction which
associates, to a morphism of posets, a functor between the corresponding path categories.

Construction 9.1.9. Let φ : A →B be a morphism of posets. We define a functor between poset-enriched
categories Pφ : PA →PB as follows.
• Pφ is equal to φ at the level of objects.
• Given a, b ∈A and a sequence a = [a = a0 < a1 < . . . < an = b] ∈PA(a, b), we define Pφ(a, b)(a) as
the ordered sequence of distinct elements of the sequence [φ(a0) ≤φ(a1) ≤. . . ≤φ(an)].
Definition 9.1.10. Let (M, A, Strat) and (N, B, Strat′) be two stratified categories. A functor of stratified
categories between these is a functor of topological categories G : M →N satisfying the following conditions.
• The map A →B induced by G at the level of objects is order-preserving. We denote the corresponding
morphism of posets by Gobj.
• The following diagram of topological categories commutes

Lemma 9.1.13. The composition of two functors of stratified categories is a functor of stratified categories.

9.2
The flow coherent nerve of a stratified category
The goal of this section is to introduce the flow coherent nerve of a stratified category, and to give conditions
under which it is an ∞-category, and under which it is an ∞-category equivalent to the homotopy coherent
nerve of the underlying topological category.
Inspired by example 9.1.14, we introduce the following definition. Recall that we denote by F : sSet →CatTop
the left adjoint of the homotopy coherent nerve functor. The functor F is studied in detail in section 4.
Definition 9.2.1. Let (M, A, Strat) be a stratified category.
The flow coherent nerve of (M, A, Strat),
denoted SM, is the simplicial subset of the homotopy coherent nerve of M whose n-simplices are the functors
of topological categories F(∆n) →M that are functors of stratified categories. Here, F(∆n) is endowed
with the structure of stratified category described in remark 9.1.7. A simplex of N(M) that belongs to SM
is called an unbroken simplex of N(M).
The fact that SM is indeed a simplicial set, i.e.,stable by face and degeneracy operators in N(M), can be
argued as follows. The structures of stratified categories on the topological categories F(∆n) are defined
in such a way that for every morphism φ : ∆n →∆m in ∆, the functor of topological categories F(φ) :
F(∆n) →F(∆m) is a functor of stratified categories. Now, if we have an unbroken m-simplex of N(M) given
by a functor of stratified categories F(∆m) →M, its image by the operator N(M)m →N(M)n induced
by φ is obtained by precomposing with F(φ). The statement follows because a composition of functors of
stratified categories is a functor of stratified categories (lemma 9.1.13).
Example 9.2.2. By example 9.1.14, the definition of the flow coherent nerve of a stratified category recovers
the definition of the flow coherent nerve of the flow category of a Morse-Smale pair.
We have the following characterization of unbroken simplices of a stratified category.
Proposition 9.2.3. Let (M, A, Strat) be a stratified category. An n-simplex σ ∈N(M)n is unbroken if
and only if the image of every unbroken morphism of F(∆n) by the functor σ : F(∆n) →M is an unbroken
morphism of M.
Proof. In order to prove that the condition is necessary, assume that σ is unbroken, consider two integers
0 ≤i ≤j ≤n and an unbroken morphism γ ∈F(∆n)(i, j). We wish to prove that σ(i, j)(γ) is unbroken.

the map hZ →Z induced from the preceding homotopy commutative diagram exhibits Z as a homotopy
pushout of (D), then the inclusion Z1 →Z is a weak homotopy equivalence.
In [Qui88, Section 2], Quinn defines Z0 to be tame in Z by a geometric condition which he proves to be
equivalent to the condition that the map hZ →Z is a (strong) homotopy equivalence ([Qui88, Lemma 2.4]).

and the starting point evaluation map Exit(Z0, Z1) →Z0 is a Hurewicz fibration. We have thus shown the
following.
Proposition 9.2.8. Let (M, A, Strat) be a stratified category. Suppose that for every a, b ∈A the topological
space M(a, b) is homotopically stratified, with respect to the (0 < 1)-stratification described in proposition
9.2.6. Then the flow coherent nerve of (M, A, Strat) is an ∞-category. Suppose additionally that for every
a, b ∈A the starting point evaluation map Exit(M(a, b)0, M(a, b)1) →M(a, b)0 is a weak homotopy equiv-
alence. Then the inclusion of the flow coherent nerve of M into the homotopy coherent nerve of M is an
equivalence of ∞-categories.
Remark 9.2.9. The conditions of proposition 9.2.8 are satisfied by the flow category of a Morse-Smale
pair on a smooth closed manifold, endowed with the structure of stratified category described in example
9.1.8. The condition that the spaces of morphisms are homotopically stratified follows from the existence of
structures of smooth manifolds with corners from proposition 2.4.41. The property about the starting point
evaluation map is part of lemma 7.1.11.
9.3
Comparison between stratified categories and exit path categories
In this section, we build upon the previous two sections to present a framework in which the methods of this
paper apply to prove that a stratified category is equivalent, as an ∞-category, to the exit path ∞-category
of some stratified space.
Suppose we are given a topological space Y , a poset A and a stratification π : Y →A. We will set up a
framework in which we can compare the flow coherent nerve of some stratified category, to the stratified
singular simplicial set of (Y, A). To begin with, we will need two further assumptions.
• We suppose that we have a strictly decreasing map f : A →R 17 (not every poset admits such a map,
see remark 9.3.1 for a discussion).
• Up to restricting ourselves to the full subposet of A spanned by the image of π, we may assume that
π is surjective, which we do. We also assume that a set-theoretic section of π is given (such a section
always exists). Such a section is the same as a choice, for every a ∈A, of a point in Ya. We identify A
with a subset of Y using this section, i.e.,we have a ∈Ya.
Remark 9.3.1. For cardinality reasons, not every poset admits a strictly decreasing map to R (or, equiva-
lently, a strictly increasing one). An example of a totally ordered set that has the same cardinality as R and
does not admit a strictly decreasing map to R is given in [HLST71, Section 2.1]. On the other hand, every
countable poset admits a strictly decreasing map to R; this is proven in [HLST71, Section 2.1, Theorem 1]
for totally ordered sets and the proof can be adapted to the case of partially ordered sets.
Inspired by Morse theory, we start by introducing a triple (Mf(Y ), A, Strat) which is a stratified category
under mild assumptions on A. The set of objects of the topological category Mf(Y ) is A and its spaces of
morphisms are the spaces of "piecewise exit paths" in the A-stratified space Y that are parametrized using
f. More precisely, the topological category Mf(Y ) is constructed as follows.
Construction 9.3.2. The set of objects of Mf(Y ) is A and for every a, b ∈A, the space of morphisms
of Mf(Y ) from a to b is the topological subspace of C0([f(b), f(a)], Y ), endowed with the compact-open
topology, formed by those paths γ satisfying the following condition: γ(f(b)) = b, γ(f(a)) = a and there
exists a strictly increasing sequence a = a0 < a1 < . . . < an = b of elements of A such that for every
17We could just as well require f to be strictly increasing; our choice is made in order to be consistent with our convention
of considering negative pseudo-gradients in Morse theory.
162


## Page 163


integer 0 ≤i < n we have γ([f(ai+1), f(ai)) ⊆Yai+1 and γ(f(ai+1)) = ai+1. If we are given c ∈A and
γ ∈Mf(Y )(a, b), γ′ ∈Mf(Y )(b, c), the composition γ′ ◦γ ∈Mf(Y )(a, c) is defined as follows: given a real
number f(c) ≤s ≤f(a), the point (γ′ ◦γ)(s) is defined as γ(s) if s ≥f(b) and γ′(s) if s ≤f(b).
Remark 9.3.3. Consider the case of a Morse-Smale pair (f, ξ) on a smooth closed manifold X, whose flow
category we denote by M. Denote the poset of critical points of f by A and endow X with the A-stratification
by the stable manifolds of ξ. Denote the restriction of f to A by f as well. Recall from construction 2.4.36 the
map M(a, b) →C0([f(b), f(a)], X) for every a, b ∈A. These maps yield a functor of topological categories
M →Mf(X).
Notation 9.3.4. Given a, b ∈A and γ ∈Mf(Y ), there exists a unique sequence a = a0 < a1 < . . . < an = b
associated with γ satisfying the conditions stated in construction 9.3.2. Carrying γ to this sequence yields a
map Stratf(Y )(a, b) : Mf(Y )(a, b) →PA(a, b).
The following lemma gives conditions under which the triple (Mf(Y ), A, Strat) is a stratified category.
Lemma 9.3.5. For every a, b ∈A we denote by [a, b] the set of those elements c ∈A such that a ≤c ≤b.
Suppose that:
(i) for every a, b ∈A, the set [a, b] is finite and
(ii) for every a ∈A, {a} is a closed subset of Y .
Then the partial order on A and the maps Stratf(Y )(a, b) from notation 9.3.4 endow Mf with the structure
of a stratified category.
Proof. We need to verify that Stratf(Y ) : Mf(Y ) →PA is a functor of topological categories. Functoriality
holds without assumptions (i) and (ii) and is straightforward. It remains to show that for every a, b ∈A, the
map Stratf(Y )(a, b) : Mf(Y )(a, b) →PA(a, b) is continuous (where the right-hand side is endowed with the
Alexandrov topology). In other words, we wish to show that for every sequence of the form a = [a = a0 <
a1 < . . . < an = b], the subset Mf(Y )(a, b)≥a ⊆Mf(Y )(a, b) is open. Let E = [a, b]\{a0, a1, . . . , an} ⊆A,
K = f(E) ⊆[f(b), f(a)] and U = X\{E} ⊆Y . We have:
Mf(Y )(a, b)≥a = {γ ∈Mf(Y )(a, b) | ∀c ∈E, γ(f(c)) ̸= c}
= {γ ∈Mf(Y )(a, b) | ∀c ∈E, γ(f(c)) /∈E}
= {γ ∈Mf(Y )(a, b) | γ(K) ⊆U}.
By the assumptions (i) and (ii), K is compact and U is open in Y . By definition of the compact-open
topology, Mf(Y )(a, b)≥a is open in Mf(Y )(a, b), as desired.

The main theorem of this section is the following.
Theorem 9.3.8. Suppose that we have a stratified category (M, A, Strat) together with a functor of topo-
logical categories G : M →Mf(Y ) satisfying the following conditions:

as well as an isomorphism of semi-simplicial sets between SingCA(Y ) and Sing′
A(Y ).
Condition (v) says that the morphism SM →N(M) is an equivalence of ∞-categories. On the other hand,
condition (iv) implies that SingCA(Y ) is an ∞-category, with the same proof as in section 7.3.
However, the morphism SM →SingCA(Y ) has no chance to be essentially surjective in general, for the same
reason as that given at the beginning of section 7.4. Namely, by construction of CA the simplices of SingCA(Y )
that are entirely contained in a given stratum are just points, and so there are as many isomorphism classes

of objects of the ∞-category SingCA(Y ) as there are points in Y . However, the set of objects of SM is A
only. We remedy this by introducing the restricted version SingCA,r(Y ) ⊆SingCA(Y ) defined to consist of
those simplices whose vertices belong to A; it is stable by inner horn filling and is therefore an ∞-category.
Now the functor SM →SingCA,r(Y ) is essentially surjective, since it is a bijection at the level of objects. It
is also fully faithful under the conditions of the theorem; this can be proven by considering the maps that it
induces between right morphism spaces, as follows.
Firstly, suppose we have two objects a, b ∈A such that a < b. Remark 9.3.6 provides a homeomorphism

Lastly, suppose given two objects a, b ∈A such that a ≰b. Then both of the right morphism spaces of SM

a weak homotopy equivalence. This completes the proof of the fact that SM →SingCA,r(Y ) is fully faithful,
and an equivalence of ∞-categories.
In order to complete the construction of a zigzag of equivalences of ∞-categories between N(M) and
SingA(Y ), we introduce the restricted versions
Sing′

SingA,r(Y ) ⊆SingA(Y ),

using the fact that the strata of Y are weakly contractible (condition (iii)). Finally, the isomorphism of

between SingCA,r(Y ) and Sing′
A,r(Y ). We have therefore shown that there exists a zigzag of equivalences of
∞-categories between N(M) and SingA(Y ). The fact that the induced equivalence of ∞-categories between
N(M) and SingA(Y ) satisfies the stated properties is proved as in the Morse case in section 8.2.

A
Colimits of simplicially enriched categories
The goal of this appendix is to give a proof of the following statement.
Proposition A.0.1. The category of small simplicial categories admits all small colimits.
Proof. Let us denote by Cat the category of small categories. Recall that we denote by CatsSet the category
of small simplicial categories, which, for us, is the category of small categories enriched over simplicial sets.
Let us denote by sCat the functor category Fun(∆op, Cat). Consider the functor
CatsSet
−→
sCat
C
7−→
[n] 7→
ß Obj
= Obj(C)
HomCn(X, Y )
= HomC(X, Y )n.
It induces an equivalence between CatsSet and the full subcategory of sCat spanned by those functors
∆op →Cat having the property that the face and degeneracy maps yield bijections at the level of objects
in Cat. It is therefore enough to prove that this property is preserved by colimits in sCat, and that sCat
admits all small colimits. By remark 2.1.2, it is enough to prove that Cat admits all small colimits, and that
the forgetful functor
Cat
−→
Set
C
7−→
Obj C
commutes with colimits. This follows from corollary 4.5.16 and its proof in [Rie17].
